Second order fractional mean-field SDEs with singular kernels and measure initial data Thanks: This work is partially supported by NNSFC grants of China (Nos. 12131019), and the German Research Foundation (DFG) through the Collaborative Research Centre(CRC) 1283/2 2021 - 317210226 “Taming uncertainty and profiting from randomness and low regularity in analysis, stochastics and their applications”.
Zimo Hao, Michael Röckner and Xicheng Zhang

Abstract
. In this paper we establish the local and global well-posedness of weak and strong solutions to second order fractional mean-field SDEs with singular/distribution interaction kernels and measure initial value, where the kernel can be Newton or Coulomb potential, Riesz potential, Biot-Savart law, etc. Moreover, we also show the stability, smoothness and the short time singularity and large time decay estimates of the distribution density. Our results reveal a phenomenon that for nonlinear mean-field equations, the regularity of the initial distribution could balance the singularity of the kernel. The precise relationship between the singularity of kernels and the regularity of initial values are calculated, which belongs to the subcritical regime in the scaling sense. In particular, our results provide a microscopic probabilistic explanation and establish a unified treatment for many physical models such as the fractional Vlasov-Poisson-Fokker-Planck system, the vorticity formulation of 2D-fractal Navier-Stokes equations, surface quasi-geostrophic models, fractional porous medium equation with viscosity, etc. Keywords : Mean-field SDEs, Anisotropic Besov spaces, Coulomb potential, Riesz potential. AMS 2010 Mathematics Subject Classification: 60H10, 35H10



1.
Introduction

In this paper we are concerned with the following second order mean-field stochastic differential equation in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
(also called McKean-Vlasov or distribution-dependent SDE) driven by standard Brownian motion
W_{t}
,

\displaystyle\ddot{X}_{t}=(b_{t}*\mu_{t})(X_{t},\dot{X}_{t})+\sqrt{2}\dot{W}_{t},\ \ (X_{0},\dot{X}_{0})\sim\mu_{0},

(1.1)

or by an
\alpha
-stable process
L^{(\alpha)}_{t}
with
\alpha\in(1,2)
, whose infinitesimal generator is given by the usual fractional Laplacian
\Delta^{\alpha/2}
,

\displaystyle\ddot{X}_{t}=(b_{t}*\mu_{t})(X_{t},\dot{X}_{t})+\dot{L}^{(\alpha)}_{t},\ \ (X_{0},\dot{X}_{0})\sim\mu_{0},

(1.2)

where
\mu_{0}\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d})
is the initial probability distribution on
{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
,

\mu_{t}
stands for the joint law of
(X_{t},\dot{X}_{t})
,
and
b
is a time-dependent
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
-valued (Schwartz) distribution over the phase space
{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
,

b_{t}*\mu_{t}
is the usual convolution of two distributions. Here we require that
b*\mu\in L^{1}_{loc}({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2d})
.
Below for the unification of notations, we shall write

L^{(2)}_{t}=\sqrt{2}W_{t}.

Note that if we introduce the velocity variable
V_{t}:=\dot{X}_{t}
, then SDEs (
1.1
) and (
1.2
) can be written as the following first-order degenerate system: for
\alpha\in(1,2]
,

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &X_{t}=X_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}V_{s}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}s,\\
&V_{t}=V_{0}+\int^{t}_{0}(b_{s}*\mu_{s})(X_{s},V_{s}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}s+L^{(\alpha)}_{t}.\end{aligned}\right.

(1.3)

It should be noted that if
b(t,x,v)=b(t,v)
does not depend on the position variable
x
, then SDE (
1.3
) reduces to the following first-order nondegenerate SDE

\displaystyle{\mathord{{\rm d}}}V_{t}=(b_{t}*\mu_{t})(V_{t})+{\mathord{{\rm d}}}L^{(\alpha)}_{t}.

(1.4)

Suppose that
\mu_{t}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}x,{\mathord{{\rm d}}}v)=f(t,x,v){\mathord{{\rm d}}}x{\mathord{{\rm d}}}v
.
Then by Itô’s formula,
f
solves the following nonlinear kinetic Fokker-Planck equation in the distributional sense:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}f+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f=\Delta^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}_{v}f-\mathord{{\rm div}}_{v}((b*f)f).

(1.5)

If
b(t,x,v)=b(t,v)
does not depend on
x
, then
\rho(t,v):=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}f(t,x,v){\mathord{{\rm d}}}x
solves the following nonlinear and nondegenerate Fokker-Planck equation:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\rho=\Delta_{v}^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}\rho-\mathord{{\rm div}}_{v}((b*\rho)\rho).

(1.6)

The aim of this work is to show the weak and strong well-posedness of SDEs (
1.3
) and
(
1.4
) with singular
b
for lowly regular initial values via studying PDEs (
1.5
) and (
1.6
).
Especially, we find a precise relationship between the singularity of the kernel
b
and the regularity of
\mu_{0}
in terms of their Besov norms, which is almost sharp and belongs to the subcritical regime in the scaling sense. In particular,
it allows kernels in (
1.6
)
like
b(x)=\nabla|x|^{s-d}
for some
s\in(2-\alpha,d)
and
\rho_{0}\in L^{1}\cap L^{p_{0}}
for some
p_{0}>\frac{d}{s-2+\alpha}
, see Remark
1.10
below.

1.1.
Background and motivation

Consider the interacting many-body system of
N
-particles in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
governed by Newtonian mechanics and stochastic noises,

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &\dot{X}^{N,i}_{t}=V^{N,i}_{t},\ \ i=1,2,\cdots,N,\\
&\dot{V}^{N,i}_{t}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j\not=i}K(X^{N,i}_{t}-X^{N,j}_{t})+\sqrt{2\varepsilon}\dot{W}^{N,i},\end{aligned}\right.

(1.7)

and subjected to i.i.d. initial value
(X^{N,i}_{0},V^{N,i}_{0})=(\xi^{i}_{0},\eta^{i}_{0})
, whose law is
absolutely continuous or singular with respect to the Lebesgue measure,
where
(X^{N,i}_{t},V^{N,i}_{t})
stands for the position and velocity of the
i
th-particle at time
t
,

K:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
models the pairwise interaction between different particles,

(\dot{W}^{N,i})_{i=1,\cdots,N}
are the generalized derivatives of
N
independent Brownian motions, which models the collision between particles and
a background medium,
\varepsilon>0
stands for the intensity of the noise, see
[
77
]
for the physical background.
In this work we are mainly interested in the interaction kernel
K=A\cdot\nabla U
(see
[
40
]
), where
A
is some
d\times d
-matrix and

\displaystyle U(x)=|x|^{-s},\ \ s\in(0,d)\ \mbox{ or }\ U(x)=\ln|x|,\ \ d=1,2.

(1.8)

Note that
U(x)=|x|^{2-d}
for
d\geqslant 3
or
U(x)=\ln|x|
for
d=2
is the classical Newton or Coulomb kernel,

U(x)=|x|^{1-d}
for
d\geqslant 2
is called the Riesz kernel.
Since it is hard to directly study the particle system (
1.7
) by observing the motion of each particle
in physical models, one usually considers the empirical measure

\mu^{N}_{t}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}x,{\mathord{{\rm d}}}v):=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{(X^{N,i}_{t},V^{N,i}_{t})}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}x,{\mathord{{\rm d}}}v).

It is assumed that
\mu^{N}_{t}
weakly converges to some measure of type
f(t,x,v){\mathord{{\rm d}}}x{\mathord{{\rm d}}}v
, which is also called
mean-field limit or propagation of chaos in Kac’s sense (see
[
50
]
,
[
40
]
and
[
18
]
), where the density
f
satisfies the
following Vlasov-Poisson-Fokker-Planck equation (abbreviated as VPFP)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\partial_{t}f+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f&=\varepsilon\Delta_{v}f-\mathord{{\rm div}}_{v}(K*\langle f\rangle f)=\varepsilon\Delta_{v}f-(K*\langle f\rangle)\cdot\nabla_{v}f.\end{split}

(1.9)

Here
\langle f\rangle(t,x):=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}f(t,x,v){\mathord{{\rm d}}}v
is the mass or charge density.
PDE (
1.9
) can be regarded as a macroscopic description of the microscopic particle system (
1.7
).

In the three dimensional case, when
\varepsilon=0
, that is, there is no viscosity in (
1.9
), and
U
is the Newton/Coulomb potential, which corresponds to gravitational/electrostatic interaction,
PDE (
1.9
) is also called Vlasov-Poisson equation and describes the evolution of the distribution density of particles in the phase space
{\mathbb{R}}^{6}
and appears in galactic dynamics and plasma physics (see
[
63
]
and references therein).
No viscosity implies that PDE (
1.9
) is a time-reversible system in the sense that
if
f
is a solution of (
1.9
), then
\tilde{f}(t,x,v):=f(-t,x,-v)
is also a solution of (
1.9
).
The existence of global smooth solutions to the Vlasov-Poisson equation
was shown in
[
59
]
,
[
66
]
,
[
70
]
under some moment assumptions on the initial value.
The existence of classical solutions to the VPFP equation was obtained in
[
10
]
,
[
14
]
,
[
64
]
by the same method as in
[
59
]
.
All the results require that the initial value
f_{0}\in L^{1}\cap L^{\infty}
has a finite second order moment in
v
, that is,

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}|v|^{2}f_{0}(x,v){\mathord{{\rm d}}}x{\mathord{{\rm d}}}v<\infty.

(1.10)

When the initial distribution
f_{0}
is a measure, say in some Morrey space, and satisfies some smallness assumptions,
the well-posedness of VPFP (
1.9
) was established in
[
14
]
(see the earlier work
[
86
]
for one dimensional case).
On the other hand, from the physical viewpoint, the mean-field limit provides an explanation for Vlasov-Poisson equations
from Newton’s second law for the motion of
N
particles. Such a problem
has been studied extensively in the context of PDEs (see
[
50
]
and
[
40
]
).
When
K
is Lipschitz continuous, the propagation of chaos for particle system (
1.7
)
was firstly shown by McKean
[
61
]
. When the interaction kernel
K
is bounded measurable, Jabin and Wang
[
51
]

showed the quantitative rate of the propagation of chaos via the entropy method. Based on the BBGKY method, Lacker
[
57
]

showed the optimal rate of convergence
by the Girsanov transform and deep analysis. For singular potentials
U
, there is also some cutoff version about the mean-field limits,
see
[
47
]
and references therein.
A complete review of mean field limits for Vlasov-Poisson equations is contained in
[
50
]
and
[
40
]
.

From the probabilistic view point, it is natural to expect that the distributional density solution
f
of VPFP (
1.9
) is the law
of the solution
Z_{t}=(X_{t},V_{t})
to the following mean-field SDE, if a solution exists in Itô’s sense:

\displaystyle\dot{X}_{t}=V_{t},\ \ \dot{V}_{t}=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}K(X_{t}-y)\mu_{X_{t}}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}y)+\sqrt{2\varepsilon}\dot{W}_{t},

(1.11)

where
\mu_{X_{t}}
stands for the probability distribution measure of
X_{t}
. As discussed above, although there are a lot of references about
the solvability of PDE (
1.9
), there are few results about SDE (
1.11
), e.g., about its
weak/strong well-posedness in the probabilistic sense. The difficulty of solving (
1.11
) lies in that DDSDE
(
1.11
) is degenerate and the drift is too singular for
K=A\cdot\nabla U
with
U
given in (
1.8
).

Besides the second order particle system (
1.7
), we are also interested in the following first order system
that is related to the point vortex system and other physical models:

\displaystyle\dot{X}^{N,i}_{t}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j\not=i}K(X^{N,i}_{t}-X^{N,j}_{t})+\sqrt{2\varepsilon}\dot{W}^{N,i}_{t}.

(1.12)

As above, it is usually assumed that the limit of the empirical measure
\mu^{N}_{t}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}x):=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{X^{N,i}_{t}}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}x)
solves the following
Fokker-Planck equation:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\rho=\varepsilon\Delta\rho-\mathord{{\rm div}}((K*\rho)\rho),

(1.13)

where
\rho
is the distribution density of the solution to the following McKean-Vlasov SDE:

\displaystyle X_{t}=X_{0}+\int^{t}_{0}(K*\mu_{X_{s}})(X_{s}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}s+\sqrt{2\varepsilon}W_{t}.

(1.14)

For the above first order particle system with
K=A\cdot\nabla U
as described in (
1.8
),
Serfaty
[
72
]
established the conditional mean field convergence
by modulated free energy. On the two dimensional torus, when
K
is the Biot-Savart law, Jabin and Wang
[
52
]

showed some quantitative rate of convergence of propagation of chaos for the point vortex system by the entropy method.
When the interaction kernel is in
L^{p}
-space with
p>d
, the strong convergence of propagation of chaos was obtained recently in
[
43
]
. The purpose of the current work is not to prove the propagation of chaos,
but concentrated on the solvability of SDEs (
1.11
) (and hence the corresponding nonlinear Fokker-Planck equations (
1.9
) and (
1.13
) respectively) and (
1.14
) with singular interaction kernels.

Before going further, we introduce more examples from physics for equation (
1.13
).

Example 1.
Consider the following vorticity form of the 2D Navier-Stokes equation:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}w=\Delta w+(K_{2}*w)\cdot\nabla w=\Delta w+\mathord{{\rm div}}((K_{2}*w)w),

(1.15)

where
K_{2}
is the Biot-Savart law given by

K_{2}(x_{1},x_{2})=(-x_{2},x_{1})/|x|^{2}.

If the initial vorticity
w_{0}
is a finite signed measure, the existence of weak solutions to equation (
1.15
)
was proved by Cottet
[
31
]
and Giga, Miyakawa and Osada
[
38
]
.
The uniqueness is a more difficult problem and was partially proved in
[
38
]
,
[
37
]
and then completely solved by
Gallagher and Gallay
[
36
]
. The proofs in
[
38
]
and
[
36
]
strongly depend on the heat kernel estimates
of
\partial_{t}-\Delta-U\cdot\nabla
established by Osada in
[
65
]
,
where
U:{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
is a divergence free vector field which satisfies

\|U(t)\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}\leqslant C/\sqrt{t},\ \ \|\partial_{2}U_{1}(t)-\partial_{1}U_{2}(t)\|_{L^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}\leqslant C.

For the classical Navier-Stokes equation of velocity fields, there are also numerous papers about the initial velocity being in
some homogeneous spaces (see
[
54
]
,
[
39
]
,
[
55
]
,
[
13
]
,
[
56
]
). It should be noted that
there is an essential discrepancy between the vorticity formulation and the original two-dimensional
Navier-Stokes equations because if
u\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
and
w=\partial_{2}u_{1}-\partial_{1}u_{2}\in L^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
, then
\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}w{\mathord{{\rm d}}}x=0
.
One of our main results shows that when
the initial vorticity is in
\cup_{\varepsilon>0}{\mathbf{B}}^{-3/2+\varepsilon}_{\infty}
, then there is a unique smooth solution to equation (
1.15
).
In particular, it includes the white noise in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
as the initial vorticity, which seems to be new.

Example 2.
Consider the following surface quasi-geostrophic equation in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
(abbreviated by SQG):
for
\alpha\in(0,2)
,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\theta=\Delta^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}\theta+({\mathscr{R}}*\theta)\cdot\nabla\theta,

(1.16)

where
{\mathscr{R}}=(-{\mathscr{R}}_{2},{\mathscr{R}}_{1})
and
{\mathscr{R}}_{i}
is the Riesz transform

{\mathscr{R}}_{i}(x)=x_{i}/|x|^{3},\ x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2},\ i=1,2.

The SQG equation appeared in the study of the mathematical theory of meteorology and oceanography
[
29
]
,
[
46
]
.
It is classified in subcritical/critical/supercritical cases according to the fractional power

\alpha\in(1,2)
/
\alpha=1
/
\alpha\in(0,1)
. In
[
30
]
,
Constantin and Wu showed that in the subcritical case any solution with smooth initial value is smooth for all time.
In both the critical case and supercritical cases, Chae and Lee
[
17
]
showed
the well-posedness of solutions with initial conditions small in Besov spaces (see also
[
80
]
). In the critical case, Cafferalli and Vaseur
[
12
]

showed the global well-posedness for SQG (
1.16
) for large initial values. All these results require that the initial value is in
L^{2}
. We are interested in the well-posedness of (
1.16
) when the initial data are measures or certain Schwartz distributions.

Example 3.
Let
d\geqslant 3
,
s\in[0,1]
and
\varepsilon\geqslant 0
.
Consider the following fractional porous medium equation in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
with viscosity:

\partial_{t}u=\varepsilon\Delta u+\mathord{{\rm div}}((\nabla\Delta^{-s}u)u).

For
s\in(0,1]
, it is well known that the nonlocal operator
\Delta^{-s}u
can be written as (see
[
73
]
)

\Delta^{-s}u=K*u,\ \ K(x):=c_{d,s}|x|^{2s-d},

where
c_{d,s}>0
is a normalizing constant.
Thus, if we take

U(x)=|x|^{2s-d}{\mathbbm{1}}_{s\in(0,1]}+\delta_{0}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}x){\mathbbm{1}}_{s=0},

then for
s\in[0,1]
,

\nabla\Delta^{-s}u=\nabla U*u.

For
s=0
and
\varepsilon=0
, it is the classical porous medium equation. For
s=1
and
\varepsilon=0
, it was investigated in
[
19
]
and

[
34
]

for the evolution of the vortex density in a superconductor. For
s\in(0,1)
and
\varepsilon=0
, it is called fractional porous medium equation and
was studied in
[
9
]
and
[
76
]
.
For
\varepsilon>0
and
s\in[0,1]
, it was considered as the viscosity approximation of fractional porous medium equation in

[
71
]
and
[
25
]
. In particular, our results can be applied to the above equation for
\varepsilon>0
. In fact, we shall consider a kinetic version, which seems not physically relevant, but appears interesting for mathematics.

1.2.
Main results

In this section we provide explicit conditions on
b
and initial conditions
so that the mean-field SDE (
1.3
) admits a unique weak/strong solution in the probabilistic sense.

To state our main results, we first introduce some notations used in this paper.
For a muti-index
{\boldsymbol{p}}=(p_{x},p_{v})\in[1,\infty]^{2}
, we define

\displaystyle\|f\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}}:=\|f\|_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}:=\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\|f(\cdot,v)\|_{p_{x}}^{p_{v}}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}v\right)^{1/p_{v}},

(1.17)

and introduce the scaling parameter
{\boldsymbol{a}}
by

{\boldsymbol{a}}:=(1+\alpha,1),\ \ \alpha\in(0,2).

For notational convenience, we shall write below

\displaystyle\frac{1}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}:=\Big(\frac{1}{p_{x}},\frac{1}{p_{v}}\Big),\ \ {\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot\frac{d}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}:=\frac{(1+\alpha)d}{p_{x}}+\frac{d}{p_{v}},

and for any
{\boldsymbol{p}},{\boldsymbol{q}}\in[1,\infty]^{2}
,

{\boldsymbol{p}}\geqslant{\boldsymbol{q}}\Leftrightarrow p_{x}\geqslant q_{x},\ p_{v}\geqslant q_{v}.

Moreover, we use bold letters to denote constant vectors in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
, for example,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{1}:=(1,1),\quad{\boldsymbol{d}}:=(d,d).

We remark that the above mixed
{\mathbb{L}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}
-norm was first introduced in
[
7
]
. Recently, it was used in an essential way in
[
43
]

to study the strong convergence of propagation of chaos for particle systems with singular interactions (see also
[
58
]
,
[
84
]
).
We emphasize that the order of
(p_{x},p_{v})
in definition (
1.17
) is important because the above
{\mathbb{L}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}
-norm
is invariant under the group action
\Gamma_{t}f(x,v)=f(x-tv,v)
associated with
\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{v}
. More precisely,
for any
{\boldsymbol{p}}\in[1,\infty]^{2}
and
f\in{\mathbb{L}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}
,

\displaystyle\|\Gamma_{t}f\|_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}=\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\|f(\cdot-tv,v)\|_{p_{x}}^{p_{v}}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}v\right)^{1/p_{v}}=\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\|f(\cdot,v)\|_{p_{x}}^{p_{v}}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}v\right)^{1/p_{v}}=\|f\|_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}.

(1.18)

Now if we let
Z_{t}:=(X_{t},V_{t})
, then SDE (
1.3
) can be written in the following compact form:

\displaystyle Z_{t}=Z_{0}+\int^{t}_{0}{\mathbf{H}}_{s}(Z_{s}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}s+\sigma L^{(\alpha)}_{t},

(1.19)

where
\sigma:=(0,{\mathbb{I}})^{*}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}\otimes{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
and for
z=(x,v)
,

\displaystyle{\mathbf{H}}_{t}(z):=(v,{H}_{t}(z))^{*}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d},\ \ {H}_{t}(z):=(b_{t}*\mu_{t})(z).

(1.20)

We first give the definitions of weak/strong solutions to mean-field SDEs (
1.19
).

Definition 1.1
.

Let
\alpha\in(1,2]
and
{\mathscr{G}}:=(\Omega,{\mathscr{F}},{\mathbf{P}};({\mathscr{F}}_{t})_{t\geqslant 0})
be a stochastic basis. Let
Z:=(Z_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}
and

L^{(\alpha)}:=(L^{(\alpha)}_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}

be two
{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
and
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
-valued cádlág
{\mathscr{F}}_{t}
-adapted processes.

(A)

(Weak solutions)
For given distribution
\mu_{0}\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d})
,
we call
(Z,L^{(\alpha)},{\mathscr{G}})
a weak solution of SDE (
1.19
) with initial distribution
\mu_{0}
if

(i)

L^{(\alpha)}
is an
\alpha
-stable process with generator
\Delta^{\alpha/2}
and
{\mathbf{P}}\circ Z^{-1}_{0}=\mu_{0}
.

(ii)

For each
t>0
,
{\mathbf{P}}\circ Z^{-1}_{t}
admits a
C^{\infty}
density
f(t,z)
so that

\displaystyle\int^{T}_{0}\sup_{z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}|{H}_{s}(z)|{\mathord{{\rm d}}}s<\infty,\ \ {H}_{t}(z):=(b_{t}*f(t))(z).

(1.21)

(iii)

For all
t>0

Z_{t}=Z_{0}+\int^{t}_{0}{\mathbf{H}}_{s}(Z_{s}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}s+\sigma L^{(\alpha)}_{t},\ \ {\mathbf{P}}-a.s.

(B)

(Strong solutions)
Let
L^{(\alpha)}
be an
\alpha
-stable process with generator
\Delta^{\alpha/2}
and

{\mathscr{F}}^{0}_{t}:={\mathscr{F}}_{0}\vee\sigma\{L^{(\alpha)}_{s},s\leqslant t\}.

For given
Z_{0}\in{\mathscr{F}}^{0}_{0}
,
we call a
{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
-value cádlág
{\mathscr{F}}^{0}_{t}
-adapted process
Z:=(Z_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}
a strong solution
of SDE (
1.19
) starting from
Z_{0}
if (ii) and (iii) above hold.

Remark 1.2
.

The above definitions are standard (for example, see
[
53
]
and
[
35
]
), where we used the convention that the
2
-stable process with generator

\Delta
is just a Brownian motion with variance
2t
.
We would like to mention that condition (
1.21
) guarantees the well-definedness of

\int^{t}_{0}{\mathbf{H}}_{s}(Z_{s}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}s
.

Our first result is the following weak/strong well-posedness about the second order mean-field SDE (
1.3
) or (
1.19
).

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
\alpha\in(1,2]
,
q_{b}\in(\frac{\alpha}{\alpha-1},\infty]

and
{\boldsymbol{p}}_{0},\boldsymbol{\varrho}_{0},\boldsymbol{\varrho}_{1}\in[1,\infty]^{2}
with
\boldsymbol{1}\leqslant\tfrac{1}{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{0}}+\tfrac{1}{\boldsymbol{\varrho}_{0}}
and
\boldsymbol{\varrho}_{0}\leqslant\boldsymbol{\varrho}_{1}
.
Let
\bar{\boldsymbol{\varrho}}_{1}\in[1,\infty]^{2}
with
\frac{1}{\boldsymbol{\varrho}_{1}}+\frac{1}{\bar{\boldsymbol{\varrho}}_{1}}=\boldsymbol{1}
and
{\boldsymbol{p}}_{1}:={\boldsymbol{p}}_{0}\wedge\bar{\boldsymbol{\varrho}}_{1}
. Define

\ {\mathscr{A}}_{i}:={\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot(\tfrac{d}{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{i}}+\tfrac{d}{\boldsymbol{\varrho}_{i}}-{\boldsymbol{d}}),\ i=0,1,\quad{\mathbb{Z}}_{\beta}:={\mathbf{B}}^{\beta}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{0};{\boldsymbol{a}}}\cap{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{1};{\boldsymbol{a}}},\ \beta\in{\mathbb{R}},

where the anisotropic Besov space
{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}:={\mathbf{B}}^{\beta,\infty}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}
is defined in Definition
2.2
below.

(A)

(Weak well-posedness)
Suppose that for some
\beta_{0}\in(\frac{\alpha}{q_{b}}-\alpha,0)

and
{\mathbb{R}}\ni\beta_{b}\not={\mathscr{A}}_{i}-\beta_{0}
,

\displaystyle 0<{\mathscr{A}}_{0}-\beta_{0}-\beta_{b}<\alpha-\tfrac{\alpha}{q_{b}}+\beta_{0}\wedge(-1),

(1.22)

and
b=b_{0}+b_{1}
, where for some
\kappa_{b}>0
,

\displaystyle\kappa_{b}:=\|b_{0}\|_{L^{q_{b}}({\mathbb{R}}_{+};{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta_{b}}_{\boldsymbol{\varrho}_{0};{\boldsymbol{a}}})}+\|b_{1}\|_{L^{q_{b}}({\mathbb{R}}_{+};{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta_{b}}_{\boldsymbol{\varrho}_{1};{\boldsymbol{a}}})}<\infty.

(1.23)

For any
\gamma_{b}>(\alpha-\frac{\alpha}{q_{b}}-1-{\mathscr{A}}_{1})\vee 0
, there is a constant
C_{0}=C_{0}(\alpha,d,\beta_{0},{\boldsymbol{p}}_{0},\beta_{b},q_{b},\gamma_{b},\boldsymbol{\varrho}_{0},\boldsymbol{\varrho}_{1})>0

such that for any
T\geqslant 1
and initial distribution
\mu_{0}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\beta_{0}}
satisfying

\displaystyle\|\mu_{0}\|_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\beta_{0}}}\leqslant C_{0}T^{-\frac{\gamma_{b}}{\alpha}}/\kappa_{b},

(1.24)

there exists a unique weak solution
Z
to mean field SDE (
1.19
) on
[0,T]

in the sense of Definition
1.1
so that
{H}\in{\mathbb{L}}^{q}_{T}({\mathbf{C}}^{\beta}_{\boldsymbol{a}})
for some
q\in(\frac{\alpha}{\alpha-1},\infty)
and
\beta>0
, where
{H}
is defined by (
1.20
)
and
{\mathbf{C}}^{\beta}_{\boldsymbol{a}}:={\mathbf{B}}^{\beta}_{\infty;{\boldsymbol{a}}}
is the anisotropic Hölder space.

(B)

(Strong well-posedness)
Suppose that for some
\beta_{0}\in(\frac{\alpha}{q_{b}}-\alpha,0)
and
{\mathbb{R}}\ni\beta_{b}\not={\mathscr{A}}_{i}-\beta_{0}
,

\displaystyle 0<{\mathscr{A}}_{0}-\beta_{0}-\beta_{b}<\tfrac{3}{2}\alpha-\tfrac{\alpha}{q_{b}}-2,

(1.25)

and
b=b_{0}+b_{1}
, where

\displaystyle b_{i}\in L^{q_{b}}({\mathbb{R}}_{+};{\mathbf{B}}^{s_{0},s_{1}}_{\boldsymbol{\varrho}_{i};x,{\boldsymbol{a}}}\cap{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta_{b}}_{\boldsymbol{\varrho}_{i};{\boldsymbol{a}}}),\ \ i=0,1,

(1.26)

and
s_{0}=1+\frac{\alpha}{2}
,
s_{1}=\frac{\alpha}{2}-1+\beta_{b}

and the mixed Besov space
{\mathbf{B}}^{s_{0},s_{1}}_{\boldsymbol{\varrho}_{i};x,{\boldsymbol{a}}}
is defined
in Definition
2.2
below. Let
\mu_{0}
be the law of the initial random variable
Z_{0}
. Then, under (
1.24
), for any

\gamma_{b}>(\alpha-\frac{\alpha}{q_{b}}-1-{\mathscr{A}}_{1})\vee 0
and
T>0
,
there is a unique strong solution
Z
to mean field SDE (
1.19
)
on
[0,T]
in the sense of Definition
1.1

so that
{H}\in{\mathbb{L}}^{q}_{T}({\mathbf{C}}^{\gamma}_{x}\cap{\mathbf{C}}^{\beta}_{\boldsymbol{a}})
for some
q\in(\frac{\alpha}{\alpha-1},\infty)
and
\gamma>\frac{1+\alpha/2}{1+\alpha}
,
\beta>1-\frac{\alpha}{2}
, where
{\mathbf{C}}^{\gamma}_{x}
is the Hölder space along the
x
-direction.

Moreover, we have:

(i)

(Smoothness of density and short time singularity estimates)
For any
t\in(0,T]
,

Z_{t}
has a smooth density
f
with short time regularity

\sup_{t\in(0,T]}\left(t^{\frac{\beta}{\alpha}}\|f(t)\|_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\beta+\beta_{0}}}\right)<\infty,\quad\text{ for any $\beta\geqslant 0$}.

(ii)

(Large time decay estimate)

Suppose
{\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot\tfrac{d}{\boldsymbol{\varrho}_{1}}>\alpha-\tfrac{\alpha}{q_{b}}-1
and
{\boldsymbol{p}}_{0}\not=1
.
Then we can take
\gamma_{b}=0
in (
1.24
), and there is a global weak solution to mean-field SDE (
1.19
) so that
for any
\beta\geqslant 0
and
i=0,1
,

\sup_{t>1}\Big(t^{\frac{{\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot({\boldsymbol{d}}-d/{\boldsymbol{p}}_{i})}{\alpha}}\|f(t)\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta,1}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{i};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\Big)<\infty.

(iii)

(Weak stability)
Suppose
that the assumptions in
(A)
hold.
Let
\widetilde{b}
be another kernel satisfying (
1.23
) and
\widetilde{\mu}_{0}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\beta_{0}}
another initial distribution
that satisfies (
1.24
).
Let
\widetilde{f}
be the distribution density of the solution
\widetilde{Z}
corresponding to
\widetilde{b}
and
\widetilde{\mu}_{0}
. Then for any
\beta\geqslant 0
,

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in(0,T]}\left(t^{\frac{\beta}{\alpha}}\|f(t)-\widetilde{f}(t)\|_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\beta+\beta_{0}}}\right)\lesssim_{C_{\beta}}\sum_{i=0,1}\|b_{i}-\widetilde{b}_{i}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{q_{b}}_{T}({\mathbf{B}}^{\beta_{b}}_{\boldsymbol{\varrho}_{i};{\boldsymbol{a}}})}+\|\mu_{0}-\widetilde{\mu}_{0}\|_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\beta_{0}}},

(1.27)

where
C_{\beta}=C_{\beta}(\alpha,d,\beta_{0},{\boldsymbol{p}}_{0},\beta_{b},q_{b},\boldsymbol{\varrho}_{0},\boldsymbol{\varrho}_{1},T,\kappa_{b})>0
.

(iv)

(Strong stability)

Suppose that the assumptions in
(B)
hold.
Let
\widetilde{b}
be another kernel satisfying (
1.26
) and
\widetilde{\mu}_{0}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\beta_{0}}
another initial distribution
that satisfies (
1.24
). Let
\widetilde{Z}
be the strong solution of SDE (
1.19
) corresponding to
\widetilde{b}
and
\widetilde{Z}_{0}\sim\widetilde{\mu}_{0}
. Then

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left(\sup_{t\in(0,T]}|Z_{t}-\widetilde{Z}_{t}|^{2}\right)\lesssim_{C}{\mathbb{E}}|Z_{0}-\widetilde{Z}_{0}|^{2}+\sum_{i=0,1}\|b_{i}-\widetilde{b}_{i}\|^{2}_{{\mathbb{L}}^{q_{b}}_{T}({\mathbf{B}}^{\beta_{b}}_{\boldsymbol{\varrho}_{i};{\boldsymbol{a}}})}+\|\mu_{0}-\widetilde{\mu}_{0}\|_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\beta_{0}}}^{2},

(1.28)

where
C=C(\alpha,d,\beta_{0},{\boldsymbol{p}}_{0},\beta_{b},q_{b},\boldsymbol{\varrho}_{0},\boldsymbol{\varrho}_{1},T,\kappa_{b})>0
.

(v)

(Global existence)
If
{\boldsymbol{p}}_{0}=\boldsymbol{1}
or
(\mathord{{\rm div}}_{v}b)^{-}=0
and
{\boldsymbol{p}}_{0}=(p_{0},p_{0})
,

\boldsymbol{\varrho}_{1}=(\varrho_{1},\varrho_{1})
, then the smallness condition (
1.24
) can be dropped.

Remark 1.4
.

If
\boldsymbol{\varrho}_{0}=\boldsymbol{\varrho}_{1}
, then
{\boldsymbol{p}}_{0}={\boldsymbol{p}}_{1}
since
{\boldsymbol{p}}_{0}\leqslant\bar{\boldsymbol{\varrho}}_{1}
by
\boldsymbol{1}\leqslant\tfrac{1}{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{0}}+\tfrac{1}{\boldsymbol{\varrho}_{0}}
.
Moreover,

(\mathord{{\rm div}}_{v}b)^{-}=0
means that for any nonnegative
g\in C^{\infty}_{c}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d})
,

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}\langle b(t,z),\nabla g(z)\rangle{\mathord{{\rm d}}}z\leqslant 0,\quad\text{for a.e. $t\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}$}.

Remark 1.5
.

Let
\mu_{0}\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d})\cap{\mathbb{Z}}_{\beta_{0}}
. Define for
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

\mu_{0}^{m}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}x,{\mathord{{\rm d}}}v):=\mu_{0}(2^{-(1+\alpha)m}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}x,2^{-m}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}v).

Then
\mu^{m}_{0}\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d})\cap{\mathbb{Z}}_{\beta_{0}}
and by (
2.10
) below, we have

\|\mu_{0}^{m}\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta_{0}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{i};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\leqslant\tfrac{2^{m({\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot(\frac{d}{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{i}}-{\boldsymbol{d}})-\beta_{0})}}{1-2^{\beta_{0}}}\|\mu_{0}\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta_{0}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{i};{\boldsymbol{a}}}},\ \ i=0,1.

From this, one sees that (
1.24
) holds for
\mu_{0}^{m}
as long as
0>\beta_{0}>{\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot(\frac{d}{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{i}}-{\boldsymbol{d}})
and
m
is large enough.

Remark 1.6
.

The condition
\beta_{0}\in\{0,{\mathscr{A}}_{i}-\beta_{b},i=0,1\}
are purely technical and can be dropped
since we can choose different
\beta_{0}^{\prime}<\beta_{0}

so that (
1.22
) still holds. This condition comes from the following Besov estimate:
for
{\boldsymbol{p}}\leqslant{\boldsymbol{p}}^{\prime}
and
\beta^{\prime}\not=\beta-{\mathscr{A}}
with
{\mathscr{A}}:={\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot(\tfrac{d}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}-\tfrac{d}{{\boldsymbol{p}}^{\prime}})
,

\|P_{t}f\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta^{\prime},1}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}^{\prime};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\lesssim_{C}(1\wedge t)^{-\frac{(\beta^{\prime}-\beta+{\mathscr{A}})\vee 0}{\alpha}}\|f\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta,\infty}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}},\ \ t>0,

where
P_{t}
is the kinetic semigroup (see Lemma
2.16
below).
Note that this estimate is stronger than the usual estimate

\|\nabla_{v}P_{t}f\|_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\leqslant C(1\wedge t)^{-\frac{1}{\alpha}}\|f\|_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}
. Indeed,

\|\nabla_{v}P_{t}f\|_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\leqslant C\|P_{t}f\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{1,1}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\leqslant C(1\wedge t)^{-\frac{1}{\alpha}}\|f\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{0,\infty}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\leqslant C(1\wedge t)^{-\frac{1}{\alpha}}\|f\|_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}.

The above estimate plays a crucial role in our proofs.

Remark 1.7
.

Let
b:{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
be a measurable function with
|b(t,z,z^{\prime})|\leqslant K_{t}(z-z^{\prime})
, where
K
satisfies
(
1.23
) with
-1<\beta_{b}<0
. If we replace
b*\mu
by

(b\circledast\mu)(z):=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}b(z,z^{\prime})\mu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z^{\prime}),

then the weak well-posedness still holds (see Remark
4.9
below). In this case, it is of course not expected that (
1.19
)
admits a smooth density except for the case where that
z\mapsto b(t,z,z^{\prime})
is smooth.

Remark 1.8
.

The stability estimates (
1.27
) and (
1.28
) provide us the possibility of showing the propagation of chaos
for the cutoff kernel
b
. For instance, consider the kernel
K(x)=\nabla|x|^{s-d}
and the cutoff kernel

K_{N}(x):=\nabla(|x|\vee N^{-\delta})^{s-d}
, where
s\in(0,d)
and
\delta>0
. For any
\varrho\in(\frac{d}{d-s},\infty]
and
\beta<\frac{d}{p}-d+s
,
by (
A.6
) in Appendix A, one sees that

\|K-K_{N}\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta}_{\varrho}}\leqslant N^{-\delta(\frac{d}{p}-d-\beta+s)}.

Thus one can consider the regularized particle system (
1.7
) with
K=K_{N}
(see
[
47
]
).

Now we sketch the main ingredience of the proof of Theorem
1.3
.
Let
P_{t}
be the kinetic semigroup associated with
\Delta^{\alpha/2}_{v}-v\cdot\nabla_{x}
(see (
2.22
) for a definition).
First of all we need to solve FPE (
1.5
) and obtain some precise asymptotic estimates in anisotropic Besov spaces.
More precisely, by Duhamel’s principle,

f(t)=P_{t}f_{0}-{\mathcal{H}}^{b}_{t}(f,f),

where

{\mathcal{H}}^{b}_{t}(f,g)=\int^{t}_{0}P_{t-s}\mathord{{\rm div}}_{v}((b_{s}*f_{s})g_{s}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}s.

The key point is to obtain a priori estimates for the bilinear form
(f,g)\mapsto{\mathcal{H}}^{b}(f,g)
in suitable spaces, and then use the contraction mapping theorem.
To achieve this goal, we first establish Schauder estimates
for the non-homogenous kinetic equation
\partial_{t}u=(\Delta^{\alpha/2}_{v}-v\cdot\nabla_{x})u+f

in time-weighted spaces (see Theorem
2.18
below). To show the smoothness and large time decay estimate for the solution,
by the semigroup property of
P_{t}
and shifting the time, the
following simple observation is important (see Lemma
3.9
below):

{\mathcal{H}}^{b}_{t+s}(f,g)=P_{s}{\mathcal{H}}^{b}_{t}(f,g)+{\mathcal{H}}^{b^{t}}_{s}(f^{t},g^{t}),\ \ s,t>0,

where
f^{t}(\cdot,x,v)=f(t+\cdot,x,v)
.
After establishing the solvability of (
1.5
), to prove
the weak well-posedness of McKean-Vlasov SDE (
1.19
),
we shall freeze the distribution-dependent drift along the smooth solution of the nonlinear Fokker-Planck equation,
and then utilize the uniqueness of linear Fokker-Planck equations. For the strong well-posedness, we use Zvonkin’s transformation to kill
the singular drift, where the freezed drift is Hölder continuous in the spatial variable, but has a singularity around the starting point
with respect to the time variable.

As a corollary, we also consider the following first order non-degenerate mean-field SDE

\displaystyle{\mathord{{\rm d}}}X_{t}=(b_{t}*\mu_{X_{t}})(X_{t}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}t+{\mathord{{\rm d}}}L^{(\alpha)}_{t},\ \alpha\in(1,2].

(1.29)

We have the following weak/strong well-posedness result.

Theorem 1.9
.

Let
\alpha\in(1,2]
,
q_{b}\in(\frac{\alpha}{\alpha-1},\infty]

and
p_{0},\varrho_{0},\varrho_{1}\in[1,\infty]
with
1\leqslant\tfrac{1}{p_{0}}+\tfrac{1}{\varrho_{0}}
and
\varrho_{0}\leqslant\varrho_{1}
.
Let
\bar{\varrho}_{1}\in[1,\infty]
with
\frac{1}{\varrho_{1}}+\frac{1}{\bar{\varrho}_{1}}=1
and

p_{1}:=p_{0}\wedge\bar{\varrho}_{1}
. Define

\ {\mathscr{A}}_{i}:=\tfrac{d}{p_{i}}+\tfrac{d}{\varrho_{i}}-d,\ i=0,1,\quad{\mathbb{Z}}_{\beta}:={\mathbf{B}}^{\beta}_{p_{0}}\cap{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta}_{p_{1}},\ \beta\in{\mathbb{R}},

where
{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta}_{p}={\mathbf{B}}^{\beta,\infty}_{p}
is the isotropic Besov space defined in Definition
2.2
below.

(A)

(Weak well-posedness)
Suppose that for some
\beta_{0}\in(\frac{\alpha}{q_{b}}-\alpha,0)
and
{\mathbb{R}}\ni\beta_{b}\not={\mathscr{A}}_{i}-\beta_{0}
,

\displaystyle 0<{\mathscr{A}}_{0}-\beta_{0}-\beta_{b}<\alpha-\tfrac{\alpha}{q_{b}}+\beta_{0}\wedge(-1),

(1.30)

and
b=b_{0}+b_{1}
, where

\displaystyle\kappa_{b}:=\|b_{0}\|_{L^{q_{b}}({\mathbb{R}}_{+};{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta_{b}}_{\varrho_{0}})}+\|b_{1}\|_{L^{q_{b}}({\mathbb{R}}_{+};{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta_{b}}_{\varrho_{1}})}<\infty.

(1.31)

For any
\gamma_{b}>(\alpha-\frac{\alpha}{q_{b}}-1-{\mathscr{A}}_{1})\vee 0
, there is a constant
C_{0}=(\alpha,d,\beta_{0},p_{0},\beta_{b},q_{b},\varrho_{0},\varrho_{1})>0

such that for any
T\geqslant 1
and initial distribution
\mu_{0}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\beta_{0}}
satisfying

\displaystyle\|\mu_{0}\|_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\beta_{0}}}\leqslant C_{0}T^{-\frac{\gamma_{b}}{\alpha}}/\kappa_{b},

(1.32)

there exists a unique weak solution
X
to mean field SDE (
1.29
) on
[0,T]
in the sense of Definition
1.1
so that
{H}\in{\mathbb{L}}^{q}_{T}({\mathbf{C}}^{\beta})
for some
q\in(\frac{\alpha}{\alpha-1},\infty)
and
\beta>0
, where
{H}_{t}(x):=(b_{t}*\mu_{X_{t}})(x)

and
{\mathbf{C}}^{\beta}:={\mathbf{B}}^{\beta}_{\infty}
is the isotropic Hölder space.

(B)

(Strong well-posedness)
Suppose that for some
\beta_{0}\in(\frac{\alpha}{q_{b}}-\alpha,0)
and
{\mathbb{R}}\ni\beta_{b}\not={\mathscr{A}}_{i}-\beta_{0}
,

\displaystyle 0<{\mathscr{A}}_{0}-\beta_{0}-\beta_{b}<\tfrac{3}{2}\alpha-\tfrac{\alpha}{q_{b}}-2.

(1.33)

For any
\gamma_{b}>(\alpha-\frac{\alpha}{q_{b}}-1-{\mathscr{A}}_{1})\vee 0
and
T\geqslant 1
,
under (
1.31
) and (
1.32
),
there is a unique strong solution
X
to mean field SDE (
1.29
) on
[0,T]
in the sense of Definition
1.1

so that
{H}\in{\mathbb{L}}^{q}_{T}({\mathbf{C}}^{\beta})
for some
q\in(\frac{\alpha}{\alpha-1},\infty)
and
\beta>1-\frac{\alpha}{2}
, where
{H}_{t}(x):=(b_{t}*\mu_{X_{t}})(x)

Moreover, we have the following:

(i)

(Smoothness of density and short time singularity estimates)
For any
t\in(0,T]
,

Z_{t}
has a smooth density
\rho(t)
with regularity that for any
\beta\geqslant 0
,

\sup_{t\in(0,T]}\left(t^{\frac{\beta}{\alpha}}\|\rho(t)\|_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\beta+\beta_{0}}}\right)<\infty.

(ii)

(Large time decay estimate)

Suppose
\tfrac{d}{\varrho_{1}}>\alpha-\tfrac{\alpha}{q_{b}}-1
and
p_{0}\not=1
. Then we can take
\gamma_{b}=0
in (
1.32
), and there is a global weak solution to mean-field SDE (
1.29
) so that
for any
\beta\geqslant 0
and
i=0,1
,

\sup_{t>1}\Big(t^{\frac{d-d/p_{i}}{\alpha}}\|\rho(t)\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta,1}_{p_{i}}}\Big)<\infty.

(iii)

(Two-sided estimates)
For any
T>0
, there are constants
C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}>0

such that for all
t\in(0,T)\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
,

\frac{C^{-1}_{1}}{t^{d/2}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{C_{2}t}}\mu_{0}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}x)\leqslant\rho(t,y)\leqslant\frac{C_{1}}{t^{d/2}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{C_{2}|x-y|^{2}}{t}}\mu_{0}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}x),\ \ \alpha=2,

and

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\frac{C_{3}^{-1}t\mu_{0}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}x)}{(t^{1/\alpha}+|x-y|)^{d+\alpha}}\leqslant\rho(t,y)\leqslant\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\frac{C_{3}t\mu_{0}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}x)}{(t^{1/\alpha}+|x-y|)^{d+\alpha}},\ \ \alpha\in(1,2).

(iv)

(Global existence)
If
p_{0}=1
or
(\mathord{{\rm div}}b)^{-}=0
, then we can drop the smallness condition (
1.32
).

Remark 1.10
.

When
p_{0}=1
, conditions (
1.30
) and (
1.33
) reduce to

\displaystyle\tfrac{d}{\varrho_{0}}+\tfrac{\alpha}{q_{b}}-\beta_{b}<\alpha-1,\ \ \tfrac{d}{\varrho_{0}}+\tfrac{\alpha}{q_{b}}-\beta_{b}<\tfrac{3\alpha}{2}-2,

(1.34)

which coincide with
(C0)
and
(C0
S
)
in
[
22
]
. In this case, under the same Besov regularity
on
b
,
(A)
and
(B)
were established by Chaudru de Raynal, Jabir and Menozzi in
[
22
]
. We assume
p_{0}\geqslant 1
, since we were interested to cover more examples from physics for which typical
p_{0}
has to be taken in
(1,\infty]
.
For example, if
b(x)=\nabla|x|^{s-d}
for some
s\in(2-\alpha,d)
and
\mu_{0}\in\cap_{\varepsilon>0}{\mathbf{B}}^{-\varepsilon}_{p_{0}}
, where
p_{0}>\frac{d}{s+\alpha-2}
, then the assumptions in Theorem
1.9
hold by (
A.5
) in Appendix A below.
Moreover, it is well known that for each
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, the Dirac measure
\delta_{x}\in{\mathbf{B}}^{0}_{1}
.
Thus one sees that for
b=(\delta_{x_{0}},\cdots,\delta_{x_{d}})
and
\mu_{0}\in\cap_{\varepsilon>0}{\mathbf{B}}^{-\varepsilon}_{p_{0}}
, where
p_{0}>\frac{d}{\alpha-1}
, the assumptions in Theorem
1.9
hold. In this case, SDE (
1.29
) is a density-dependent or Nemyskii’s type SDEs.

Remark 1.11
.

In this remark we make simple regularity analysis to illustrate the sharpness of condition (
1.30
) for
\beta_{0}\in[-1,0)
,
which belongs to
the subcritical regime. If the strict inequality in (
1.30
) is an equality, then it is called critical.
First of all, we consider the following SDE with linear drift

\displaystyle{\mathord{{\rm d}}}X_{t}=H_{t}(X_{t}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}t+{\mathord{{\rm d}}}L^{(\alpha)}_{t},\ \ X_{0}\sim\mu_{0},

(1.35)

where
H\in L^{q}_{t}L^{p}_{x}
for some
q,p\in[1,\infty]
.
By simple scaling analysis, it is well known that for SDE (
1.35
) or the associated Kolmogorov equations to be uniquely solvable,
even though
\mu_{0}
is smooth, we have to require (see
[
82
]
)

\displaystyle\tfrac{d}{p}+\tfrac{\alpha}{q}<\alpha-1.

(1.36)

In other words, for linear SDE (
1.35
), the regularity of initial values does not affect the singularity of
H
.
Now we consider the following first-order DDSDE with convolution drift:

\displaystyle{\mathord{{\rm d}}}X_{t}=b_{t}*\mu_{t}(X_{t}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}t+{\mathord{{\rm d}}}L^{(\alpha)}_{t},\ \ X_{0}\sim\mu_{0},

(1.37)

where
\mu_{t}
is the law of
X_{t}
.
Suppose that for some
\beta_{0},\beta_{b}\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
q_{b},p_{b},p_{0}\in[1,\infty]
,

\displaystyle b\in L^{q_{b}}({\mathbb{R}}_{+};{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta_{b}}_{p_{b}}),\ \ \mu_{0}\in{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta_{0}}_{p_{0}}.

(1.38)

We want to seek reasonable conditions on
q_{b},\beta_{b},p_{b}
and
\beta_{0},p_{0}
so that SDE (
1.37
) is uniquely solvable.
By Itô’s formula,
\mu_{t}
solves the Fokker-Planck equation
in the distributional sense:

\partial_{t}\mu=\Delta^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}\mu-\mathord{{\rm div}}((b*\mu)\mu).

Let
P_{t}
be the semigroup associated with
\Delta^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}
.
By Duhamel’s formula,

\displaystyle\mu_{t}=P_{t}\mu_{0}-\int^{t}_{0}P_{t-s}\mathord{{\rm div}}((b_{s}*\mu_{s})\mu_{s}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}s.

(1.39)

For any
p_{1}\geqslant p_{0}
and
\gamma\geqslant\frac{d}{p_{1}}-\frac{d}{p_{0}}
, by the regularization effect of
P_{t}
(see (
2.33
) below), we have

\|P_{t}\mu_{0}\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\gamma+\beta_{0}}_{p_{1}}}\leqslant C(1\wedge t)^{-(\gamma+\frac{d}{p_{0}}-\frac{d}{p_{1}})/\alpha}\|\mu_{0}\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta_{0}}_{p_{0}}}.

In particular, for any
T>0
, without considering the second term in (
1.39
),

\mu
at most belongs to
L^{q_{1}}_{T}({\mathbf{B}}^{\gamma+\beta_{0}}_{p_{1}})
for any
(q_{1},p_{1},\gamma)\in{\mathscr{I}}
,

(1.40)

where

\displaystyle{\mathscr{I}}:=\Big\{(q_{1},p_{1},\gamma):p_{1}\geqslant p_{0},\ 0\leqslant\gamma+\tfrac{d}{p_{0}}-\tfrac{d}{p_{1}}<\tfrac{\alpha}{q_{1}}\Big\}.

(1.41)

Moreover, under (
1.38
), for any
(q_{1},p_{1},\gamma)\in{\mathscr{I}}
, by Young’s convolution inequality,

{H}_{t}(x):=b_{t}*\mu_{t}(x)\in L^{q}_{T}({\mathbf{B}}^{\beta}_{p}),

where
(\beta,q,p)
is defined by

\displaystyle\beta=\beta_{b}+\gamma+\beta_{0},\quad\tfrac{1}{q}=\tfrac{1}{q_{b}}+\tfrac{1}{q_{1}},\quad 1+\tfrac{1}{p}=\tfrac{1}{p_{b}}+\tfrac{1}{p_{1}}.

(1.42)

After this analysis,
in order to make sense for
\int^{T}_{0}H_{s}(X_{s}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}s
and to solve SDE (
1.35
),
it is reasonable to assume
{H}_{t}(x)\in L^{q}_{T}(L^{p})

for some
q,p
satisfying (
1.36
). Thus by (
1.42
) with
\beta>0
, (
1.41
) and (
1.36
), we have

-\beta_{0}-\beta_{b}<\gamma<\tfrac{\alpha}{q_{1}}+\tfrac{d}{p_{1}}-\tfrac{d}{p_{0}}=\tfrac{\alpha}{q}-\tfrac{\alpha}{q_{b}}+d+\tfrac{d}{p}-\tfrac{d}{p_{b}}-\tfrac{d}{p_{0}}<\alpha-1-\tfrac{\alpha}{q_{b}}+d-\tfrac{d}{p_{b}}-\tfrac{d}{p_{0}}.

This is the case of (
1.30
) for
\beta_{0}\in[-1,0)
.
However, for
\beta_{0}\in(\frac{\alpha}{q_{b}}-\alpha,-1)
, at this moment, we do not know whether the following condition that comes from (
1.30
)

2\beta_{0}-\beta_{b}<\alpha-\tfrac{\alpha}{q_{b}}+d-\tfrac{d}{p_{b}}-\tfrac{d}{p_{0}}

is sharp. Of course, for a smaller differentiability index
\beta_{0}
, the initial value could be more singular.
We will study this in a forthcoming work.

1.3.
Related works

The study of kinetic equations has a long history. These are used to describe the evolution of the distribution density
of a single particle that is characterized by Newton’s second law in gas theory, aerodynamics, plasma physics and
non-equilibrium statistical physics (see
[
15
]
,
[
50
]
,
[
40
]
).
In 1872, Boltzmann put forward the fundamental kinetic equation of gas theory, which is a non-linear integro-differential equation,
now named Boltzmann equation, that is,

\partial_{t}f+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f=Q(f,f),

where
Q(f,f)
is the collision operator (see
[
16
]
).
It describes the motion of the molecules as a certain random process determined by collisions between pairs of molecules (see
[
16
]
).

The two-sided estimates of the heat kernel of kinetic operator
{\mathcal{K}}:=\Delta_{v}-v\cdot\nabla_{x}+b\cdot\nabla_{x}
was studied in
[
32
]
for Lipschitz drift
b
and in
[
24
]
for Hölder drift
b
.
The
L^{p}
-regularity estimate of kinetic operators
{\mathcal{K}}
was shown in Bouchut
[
11
]
.
For the fractional kinetic operator
{\mathcal{K}}^{(\alpha)}:=\Delta^{\alpha/2}_{v}-v\cdot\nabla_{x}+b\cdot\nabla_{x}
,
the
L^{p}
-maximal regularity estimates were established in
[
27
]
and
[
48
]

(see also
[
33
]
), the Schauder estimates were obtained in
[
44
]
and
[
49
]
. In fact, our proofs about the solvability of FPE (
1.5
)
strongly depend on the method developed in
[
44
]
.

Consider now the following McKean-Vlasov or distribution-dependent SDE

\displaystyle{\mathord{{\rm d}}}X_{t}=b(t,X_{t},\mu_{X_{t}}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}t+\sigma(t,X_{t},\mu_{X_{t}}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}W_{t}.

(1.43)

When
b
and
\sigma
are Lipschitz continuous in
x
and in the unknown distribution
\mu
with respect to the Euclidean distance and
Wasserstein metric, it is by now standard to show the existence and uniqueness of strong solutions to DDSDE (
1.43
) (cf.
[
74
]
and
[
18
]
).
Moreover, under some one-sided Lipschitz assumptions, Wang
[
78
]

showed the strong well-posedness and some functional inequalities for DDSDE (
1.43
). For coefficients
b,\sigma
only measurable in the measure variable, hence also containing the case of distribution density dependent (also called Nemytskii-type) coefficients, existence and uniqueness of weak solutions was proved in a series of papers (see
[
2
,
3
,
4
,
5
,
6
]
) and in the case of multiplicative Lévy noise
[
67
]
.
In
[
41
]
, Hammersley, Siska and Szpruch proved the existence of weak solutions to SDE (
1.43
) on a domain
with continuous and unbounded coefficients under Lyapunov-type conditions. Moreover, uniqueness is also obtained under some functional Lyapunov conditions.
When the diffusion matrix is uniformly non-degenerate and are only measurable and of at most linear growth, by using the classical Krylov estimates, Mishura and Veretennikov
[
62
]
showed the existence of weak solutions.
The uniqueness is also proved when the diffusion coefficient does not depend on the unknown distribution
\mu

and is Lipschitz continuous in
x
and
b
is Lipschitz continuous with respect to
\mu
.
When
b(x,\mu)=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\bar{b}(x,y)\mu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}x)
with
|\bar{b}(x,y)|\leqslant h(x-y)
, where
h\in L^{p}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
for some
p>d
,
and
\sigma(x,\mu)=\sigma(x)
does not depend on the distribution and is uniformly elliptic and Hölder continuous,
the well-posedness of DDSDE (
1.43
) was shown in
[
68
]
.
More recently, Chaudru de Raynal, Jabir and Menozzi
[
22
]
studied the well-posedness to the following DDSDE
with convolution drift

{\mathord{{\rm d}}}X_{t}=(b_{t}*\mu_{X_{t}})(X_{t}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}t+{\mathord{{\rm d}}}L^{(\alpha)}_{t}.

Under (
1.34
), for
b\in L^{q_{b}}_{T}({\mathbf{B}}^{\beta_{b}}_{p_{b}})
and any initial distribution
\mu_{0}
,
by smoothing
b
and a duality method, they showed the weak/strong
well-posedness to the above SDE. Therein they also obtained some Hölder regularity of the solution. Later, Han in
[
42
]
improved it to the smooth solution under some conditions of
b
.

Next consider the following linear second order kinetic SDE:

\displaystyle\ddot{X}_{t}=H_{t}(X_{t},\dot{X}_{t})+\dot{L}^{(\alpha)}_{t},\ \ (X_{0},\dot{X}_{0})\sim\mu_{0},

(1.44)

where
H
is only a function of
(t,x,v)
. When
\alpha=2
and
H
is
2/3+\varepsilon
-Hölder continuous in
x
and

\varepsilon
-Hölder continuous in
v
, the strong well-posedness was established in
[
20
]
and
[
79
]
.
The
L^{p}
-type drift was considered in
[
83
]
. In
[
21
]
, Chaudru de Raynal and Menozzi showed the weak well-posedness when

H\in L^{q}_{t}L^{p}_{x,v}
, where
\frac{4d}{p}+\frac{2}{q}<1
. For
\alpha\in(1,2)
, the strong well-posedness for kinetic SDE (
1.44
)
was established in
[
44
]
when
H\in L^{\infty}_{t}(C^{\gamma,\beta}_{x,v})
, where
\gamma\in(\frac{2+\alpha}{2(1+\alpha)},1)
and
\beta>1-\frac{\alpha}{2}
. It seems that there are few results about the well-posedness of kinetic DDSDEs with singular interaction kernels. The aim of this paper is to fill this gap.

After the contents of this paper was presented in a talk of “The
Non-local operators, probability and singularities”
online seminar, Stephane Menozzi informed us in a very kind e-mail
that he with P.E. Chaudru de Raynal and J.F. Jabir have a paper in
preparation with related results and sent us a current (draft) version of it
(
[
23
]
). We would like to thank Stephane very much for
this. After a detailed discussion with Stephane, we emphasize the following differences between our and their work.

•

The first and foremost difference concerns the motivation and the models in applications. In their paper, they continue the research from their very interesting previous work
[
22
]
and consider non-degenerate McKean-Vlasov equations where the initial data is a real function, i.e.
\beta_{0}\geqslant 0
, while in our present paper, we consider degenerate kinetic equations with singular kernels and measure initial data, which can be deduced naturally from the second order
N
-particle system (
1.7
). As a consequence, our results can be applied to non-degenerate equations (see Theorem
1.9
).

•

The key condition (
1.30
) is not the same as (
C
1
) in
[
23
]
. More precisely, there is only one
\beta_{b}
in our condition (
1.30
). But the condition (
C
1
) in
[
23
]
includes
2\beta_{b}
. Moreover, in
[
23
]

\beta_{0}\geqslant 0
is required, whereas
\beta_{0}<0
is allowed in our paper. It should be noted that our condition is sharp in the scaling sense (see Remark
1.11
).
Furthermore, in the PDE part, we cover the critical case in Theorem
3.11
, where the key point is that in our Schauder estimate Theorem
2.18
the norms in the left and right hand sides are
{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta^{\prime},1}_{p^{\prime}}
and
{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta,\infty}_{p}
, respectively.

•

Another important difference is in the methods of showing the well-posedness of the mean-field SDEs. In
[
23
]
, the authors mollify the singular interaction kernel and show some uniform estimate for the solution to the nonlinear Fokker-Planck equation. Then, by the weak convergence method
they obtain the existence of a solution in the sense of a martingale problem.
In our work, we use the contraction mapping theorem to show the well-posedness of the nonlinear Fokker-Planck equation and obtain
the well-posedness of the linearized SDE. Then, based on the uniqueness of the linear Fokker-Planck equation,
the solution to the linearized SDE is just the solution to the mean-field SDE (see Section
4.2
for more details).

•

For concrete physical models like the Navier-Stokes equations, in
[
23
]
, in order to avoid the non-integrability at infinity
the authors cut off the kernel by an indicator function of a bounded domain, whereas, in our work, we consider the kernel being the sum of two singular kernels so that we can cover
the physical interaction kernel in the whole space.

•

In the current work, we also pay much attention to the smoothness, stability, and large-time behavior of solutions to nonlinear Fokker-Planck equations, which are not considered in
[
23
]
. In fact, there are many techniques developed to study these aspects. For instance, we use a time shift trick to obtain the smoothness and large time decay estimates of solutions to nonlinear Fokker-Planck equations. Moreover, by choosing suitable parameters, based on an interpolation technique, we can drop the smallness assumption of
\|u_{0}\|_{{\mathcal{M}}}
in the PDE’s literature,
which is important to study the related mean-field SDEs, see Remark
5.3
below.

•

Finally, there are also some differences in the techniques. For instance, for the treatment of the convolution
b*u
,
it is regarded as an inner product
\langle b(x-\cdot),u(\cdot)\rangle
in
[
23
]
.
In our paper, we use Young’s inequality to estimate it. Moreover, we work with the kinetic operator and in
anisotropic Besov spaces, which are more difficult to handle, since for instance
{\mathcal{R}}_{j}^{a}P_{t}\neq P_{t}{\mathcal{R}}_{j}^{a}
. To obtain the
well-posedness of the equations with large initial data locally in time in the critical case, we show an asymptotic estimate for small time
for the semigroup
P_{t}
, see Lemma
3.15
.

Summarizing the above, in our opinion
[
23
]
is a very valuable contribution to the subject containing various interesting and different focal points in comparison to our paper.

1.4.
Structure of the paper

This paper is organized as follows. In Section 2 we introduce the anisotropic Besov spaces as well as some of their important properties.
Most of the results are well known and can be found in
[
75
]
. Moreover, we also prepare the basic Schauder estimates
for the model kinetic equation in time-weighted spaces (see Theorem
2.18
).

In Section 3, we study the global and local well-posedness for nonlinear kinetic Fokker-Planck equation (
1.5
)
with small or large initial data.
Moreover, we also investigate the smoothness, stability and large time decay estimates of the solution.
More precisely, in Subsection 3.1, we prove our main result Theorem
3.11
about the global well-posedness for small initial values
for the nonlinear kinetic Fokker-Planck equation (
1.5
). In Lemma
3.5
, we establish a priori bilinear
estimate for
{\mathcal{H}}^{b}_{t}(f,g)
, where Young’s inequality for convolutions provides all the regularity induced by the kernel.
In particular, the dependence on parameters is calculated carefully.
Then in Lemma
3.9
, we show the core estimate in the solution space.
In Subsection 3.2, we consider large time decay estimates.
In Subsection 3.3, we show the local well-posedness for large initial values in Theorem
3.17
.
In Subsection 3.4, we prove two
results about the global well-posedness for (
1.5
) for arbitrary large initial value.
The corresponding results for non-degenerate fractional Fokker-Planck equations are also presented.

In Section 4 we devote to the study of mean-field kinetic SDEs and prove Theorems
1.3
and
1.9
.
In Subsection 4.1, we first show the weak and strong well-posedness for linear kinetic SDEs with Hölder drifts and
driven by
\alpha
-stable processes by Zvonkin’s transformation. In particular, for strong well-posedness, we need to
assume the drift to be in
L^{q}_{T}({\mathbf{C}}^{\frac{\alpha+\beta}{1+\alpha}}_{x}\cap{\mathbf{C}}^{\beta}_{v})
, where

q\in(\frac{\alpha}{\alpha-1},\infty]
and
\beta>1-\frac{\alpha}{2}
.
Note that for
q=\infty
, this has already been obtained in
[
44
]
.
In Subsection 4.2, we prove our main theorems by freezing the drifts.

In Section 5 we study the well-posedness of fractional Vlasov-Poisson-Fokker-Planck equation (
1.9
)
with measures as initial data and the associated kinetic McKean-Vlasov SDEs. We also obtain an optimal large time decay estimate when the initial data are small.
For Wiener noise in three dimensional case, when the initial data have finite second order moment,
using well-known results, we prove global existence of smooth solutions for large initial values.
In the fractional case, the global well-posedness of (
1.9
) for large initial values seems to be open since there is no finite second order moment (see (
1.10
)), which is crucial to show global well-posedness in
[
10
,
14
,
64
]
.

In Section 6, we apply our main results to the vorticity formulation of fractional Navier-Stokes and subcritical SQG equations.
For two (and three) dimensional fractional Navier-Stokes equation, we show the global existence of smooth solutions when the initial vorticity is in
{\mathbf{B}}^{-(1+\alpha)/2+}_{\infty}
(and small). Moreover, in the two dimensional case,
we show the weak and strong well-posedness for the associated mean-field SDEs.
For the three dimensional case, we also obtain an optimal large time decay estimate. To the best of our knowledge, they are new, at least for fractional Navier-Stokes equations.

In Section 7 we present an application to fractional kinetic porous medium equations with viscosity and show the global existence
of smooth solutions for large initial values.

We conclude this introduction by introducing the following convention:
Throughout this paper, we use
C
with or without subscripts to denote constants, whose values
may change from line to line. We also use
:=
to indicate a definition and set

a\wedge b:=\max(a,b),\ \ a\vee b:={\mathord{{\rm min}}}(a,b),\ \ a^{+}:=0\vee a.

By
A\lesssim_{C}B
and
A\asymp_{C}B

or simply
A\lesssim B
and
A\asymp B
, we respectively mean that for some constant
C\geqslant 1
,

A\leqslant CB,\ \ C^{-1}B\leqslant A\leqslant CB.

Below we collect some frequently used notations for the readers’ convenience.

•

{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{R}}^{N}):=
set of all probability measures on
{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
.

•

{\mathcal{M}}({\mathbb{R}}^{N}):=
set of all finite Radon measures on
{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
.

•

\|\cdot\|_{{\mathcal{M}}}:=
total variation norm.

•

{\mathbf{B}}^{s,q}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}:=
anisotropic Besov space.

•

{\mathbf{B}}^{s,q}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}:=
isotropic Besov space.

•

{\mathbf{C}}^{s}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}:=
anisotropic Hölder-Zygmund space.

•

{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}:=
anisotropic block operator.

•

P_{t}:=
semigroup of
\Delta^{\alpha/2}_{v}-v\cdot\nabla_{x}
or
\Delta^{\alpha/2}
(see (
2.22
) below).

•

{\mathbb{X}}_{\beta},{\mathbb{Y}}_{\beta},{\mathbb{Z}}_{\beta}:=
some subspaces of Besov spaces (see (
3.8
) below).

•

{\mathbb{S}}_{\beta}:=
space of solutions (see (
3.21
) below).

2.
Anisotropic Besov space and kinetic semigroup

In this section we first recall the definition of anisotropic Besov space with mixed integrability indices as well as its
basic properties (see
[
75
]
and
[
85
]
).
Then we study the fractional kinetic semigroup and
prove time-weighted Schauder’s type estimates in anisotropic Besov spaces
for fractional kinetic operators. In order to study the small time singularity and large time decay estimates,
we introduce a time-weighted
L^{p}
-space by considering different weights for small time and large time.

2.1.
Anisotropic Besov spaces

For an
L^{1}
-integrable function
f
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
, let
\hat{f}
be the Fourier transform of
f
defined by

\hat{f}(\xi):=(2\pi)^{-d}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}\mathrm{e}^{-{\rm i}\xi\cdot z}f(z){\mathord{{\rm d}}}z,\quad\xi\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d},

and
\check{f}
the Fourier inverse transform of
f
defined by

\check{f}(z):=(2\pi)^{-d}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}\mathrm{e}^{{\rm i}\xi\cdot z}f(\xi){\mathord{{\rm d}}}\xi,\quad z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}.

Recall
{\boldsymbol{a}}=(1+\alpha,1)
. For
z=(x,v)
and
z^{\prime}=(x^{\prime},v^{\prime})
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
, we introduce the anisotropic distance

|z-z^{\prime}|_{\boldsymbol{a}}:=|x-x^{\prime}|^{1/(1+\alpha)}+|v-v^{\prime}|.

Note that
z\mapsto|z|_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}
is not smooth.
For
r>0
and
z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
, we also introduce the ball centered at
z
and with radius
r
with respect to the above distance
as follows:

B^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{r}(z):=\{z^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}:|z^{\prime}-z|_{\boldsymbol{a}}\leqslant r\},\ \ B^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{r}:=B^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{r}(0).

Let
\chi^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{0}
be a symmetric
C^{\infty}
-function on
{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
with

\chi^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{0}(\xi)=1\ \mathrm{for}\ \xi\in B^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{1}\ \mathrm{and}\ \chi^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{0}(\xi)=0\ \mathrm{for}\ \xi\notin B^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{2}.

We define

\phi^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}(\xi):=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\chi^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{0}(2^{-j{\boldsymbol{a}}}\xi)-\chi^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{0}(2^{-(j-1){\boldsymbol{a}}}\xi),\ \ &j\geqslant 1,\\
&\chi^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{0}(\xi),\ \ &j=0,\end{aligned}\right.

where for
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
\xi=(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})
,

2^{s{\boldsymbol{a}}}\xi=(2^{s(1+\alpha)}\xi_{1},2^{s}\xi_{2}).

Note that

\displaystyle{\rm supp}(\phi^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j})\subset\big\{\xi:2^{j-1}\leqslant|\xi|_{\boldsymbol{a}}\leqslant 2^{j+1}\big\},\ j\geqslant 1,\ {\rm supp}(\phi^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{0})\subset B^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{2},

(2.1)

and

\displaystyle\sum_{j\geqslant 0}\phi^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}(\xi)=1,\ \ \forall\xi\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}.

(2.2)

Let
{\mathcal{S}}
be the space of all Schwartz functions on
{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
and
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}
the dual space of
{\mathcal{S}}
, called the tempered distribution space.
For given
j\geqslant 0
, the dyadic anisotropic block operator
\mathcal{R}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}
is defined on
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}f(z):=(\phi^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}\hat{f})\check{\ }(z)=\check{\phi}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}*f(z),

(2.3)

where the convolution is understood in the distributional sense and by scaling,

\displaystyle\check{\phi}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}(z)=2^{(j-1)(2+\alpha)d}\check{\phi}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{1}(2^{(j-1){\boldsymbol{a}}}z),\ \ j\geqslant 1.

(2.4)

For
j\in{\mathbb{N}}
, by definition it is easy to see that

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}={\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j},\ \mbox{ where }\ \widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}:=\sum_{|i-j|\leqslant 2}{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{i},

(2.5)

where we used the convention that
{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{i}:=0
for
i<0
. Moreover,
by the symmetry of
\phi^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}
,

\langle{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}f,g\rangle=\langle f,{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}g\rangle,\ \ f\in{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime},\ g\in{\mathcal{S}}.

Similarly, we can define the isotropic block operator
{\mathcal{R}}_{j}f=\check{\phi}_{j}*f
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, where

\displaystyle{\rm supp}(\phi_{j})\subset\big\{\xi:2^{j-1}\leqslant|\xi|\leqslant 2^{j+1}\big\},\ j\geqslant 1,\ {\rm supp}(\phi_{0})\subset B_{2}.

(2.6)

The following Bernstein inequality is standard and proven in
[
85
]
. We omit the details.

Lemma 2.1

(Bernstein’s inequality)
.

For any
{\boldsymbol{k}}:=(k_{1},k_{2})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{2}_{0}
,
{\boldsymbol{p}}\leqslant{\boldsymbol{p}}^{\prime}\in[1,\infty]^{2}
, there is a constant
C=C({\boldsymbol{k}},{\boldsymbol{p}},{\boldsymbol{p}}^{\prime},\alpha,d)>0
such that for all
j\geqslant 0
,

\displaystyle\|\nabla^{k_{1}}_{x}\nabla^{k_{2}}_{v}{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}f\|_{{\boldsymbol{p}}^{\prime}}\lesssim_{C}2^{j{\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot({\boldsymbol{k}}+\frac{d}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}-\frac{d}{{\boldsymbol{p}}^{\prime}})}\|{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}f\|_{{\boldsymbol{p}}},

(2.7)

where
\|\cdot\|_{\boldsymbol{p}}
is defined in (
1.17
).

Now we introduce the following anisotropic Besov spaces (cf.
[
75
, Chapter 5]
).

Definition 2.2
.

Let
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
q\in[1,\infty]
and
{\boldsymbol{p}}\in[1,\infty]^{2}
. The anisotropic Besov space is defined by

\mathbf{B}^{s,q}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}:=\left\{f\in{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}:\|f\|_{\mathbf{B}^{s,q}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}:=\left(\sum_{j\geqslant 0}\big(2^{js}\|{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}f\|_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\big)^{q}\right)^{1/q}<\infty\right\},

where
\|\cdot\|_{\boldsymbol{p}}
is defined in (
1.17
).
Let
(s_{0},s_{1})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
. The mixed Besov space is defined by

{\mathbf{B}}^{s_{0},s_{1}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};x,{\boldsymbol{a}}}:=\left\{f\in{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}:\|f\|_{\mathbf{B}^{s_{0},s_{1}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};x,{\boldsymbol{a}}}}:=\sup_{k,j\geqslant 0}2^{\frac{ks_{0}}{1+\alpha}}2^{js_{1}}\|{\mathcal{R}}^{x}_{k}{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}f\|_{\boldsymbol{p}}<\infty\right\},

where for a function
f:{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}\to{\mathbb{R}}
,

{\mathcal{R}}^{x}_{k}f(x,v):={\mathcal{R}}_{k}f(\cdot,v)(x).

Moreover, we also define

{\mathbf{B}}^{s}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};x}:={\mathbf{B}}^{s,\infty}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};x}:=\left\{f\in{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}:\|f\|_{\mathbf{B}^{s}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};x}}:=\sup_{k\geqslant 0}2^{ks}\|{\mathcal{R}}^{x}_{k}f\|_{\boldsymbol{p}}<\infty\right\}.

Similarly, one defines the usual isotropic Besov spaces
{\mathbf{B}}^{s,q}_{p}

in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
in terms of isotropic block operators
{\mathcal{R}}_{j}
. If there is no confusion, we shall write

{\mathbf{B}}^{s}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}:={\mathbf{B}}^{s,\infty}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}},\ \ {\mathbf{B}}^{s}_{p}:={\mathbf{B}}^{s,\infty}_{p}.

Remark 2.3
.

The mixed Besov space
{\mathbf{B}}^{s_{0},s_{1}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};x,{\boldsymbol{a}}}
shall be used in the study of strong solutions of kinetic SDEs.
Note that for any
f\in{\mathbf{B}}^{s,q}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}
, by (
2.2
) we have

\displaystyle f=\sum_{j\geqslant 0}{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}f\ \mbox{ in ${\mathbf{B}}^{s,q}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}$.}

(2.8)

Remark 2.4
.

Any finite Radon measure
\mu
belongs to
{\mathbf{B}}^{0,\infty}_{\boldsymbol{1};{\boldsymbol{a}}}
. Indeed,
by definition and (
2.4
),

\displaystyle\|\mu\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{0,\infty}_{\boldsymbol{1};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}=\sup_{j\geqslant 0}\|{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}\mu\|_{\boldsymbol{1}}\leqslant\sup_{j\geqslant 0}\|\check{\phi}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}\|_{\bf 1}\|\mu\|_{\mathcal{M}}=\Big(\|\check{\phi}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{0}\|_{1}\vee\|\check{\phi}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{1}\|_{1}\Big)\|\mu\|_{\mathcal{M}}<\infty,

(2.9)

where
\|\mu\|_{\mathcal{M}}
stands for the norm of total variation.
Moreover, for any
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
, if we define

\mu_{m}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z):=\mu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}(2^{-m{\boldsymbol{a}}}z)),

then for
j\geqslant 1
, by scaling
\check{\phi}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}(2^{m{\boldsymbol{a}}}z)=2^{-m(2+\alpha)d}\check{\phi}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{m+j}(z)
, it is easy to see that

{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}\mu_{m}(z)=2^{-m(2+\alpha)d}({\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j+m}\mu)(2^{-m{\boldsymbol{a}}}z).

Hence, for any
{\boldsymbol{p}}\in[1,\infty]^{2}
,

\|{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}\mu_{m}\|_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}=2^{m{\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot(\frac{d}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}-{\boldsymbol{d}})}\|{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j+m}\mu\|_{{\boldsymbol{p}}},\ j\geqslant 1.

Moreover, for
j=0
, noting that

{\mathscr{F}}(\check{\phi}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{0}(2^{m{\boldsymbol{a}}}\cdot))(\xi)=2^{-m(2+\alpha)d}\phi^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{0}(2^{-m{\boldsymbol{a}}}\xi)

and

\phi^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{0}(2^{-m{\boldsymbol{a}}}\xi)=\phi^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{0}(2^{-m{\boldsymbol{a}}}\xi)\sum_{j=0}^{m}\phi^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}(\xi),

we have

\check{\phi}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{0}(2^{m{\boldsymbol{a}}}\cdot)=\sum_{j=0}^{m}\check{\phi}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{0}(2^{m{\boldsymbol{a}}}\cdot)*\check{\phi}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\|{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{0}\mu_{m}\|_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}

\displaystyle=2^{m{\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot\frac{d}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}\|\check{\phi}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{0}(2^{m{\boldsymbol{a}}}\cdot)*\mu\|_{\boldsymbol{p}}\leqslant 2^{m{\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot\frac{d}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}\sum_{j=0}^{m}\|\check{\phi}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{0}(2^{m{\boldsymbol{a}}}\cdot)*{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}\mu\|_{\boldsymbol{p}}

\displaystyle\leqslant 2^{m{\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot\frac{d}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}\|\check{\phi}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{0}(2^{m{\boldsymbol{a}}}\cdot)\|_{\boldsymbol{1}}\sum_{j=0}^{m}\|{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}\mu\|_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\leqslant 2^{m{\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot(\frac{d}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}-{\boldsymbol{d}})}\left[2{\mathbbm{1}}_{s>0}+\tfrac{2^{-(m+1)s}-1}{2^{-s}-1}{\mathbbm{1}}_{s<0}\right]\|\mu\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{s,\infty}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}.

Combining the above calculations, we obtain that for any
s\in{\mathbb{R}}\backslash\{0\}
and
{\boldsymbol{p}}\in[1,\infty]^{2}
,

\displaystyle\|\mu_{m}\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{s,\infty}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\leqslant 2^{m{\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot(\frac{d}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}-{\boldsymbol{d}})}\left[2{\mathbbm{1}}_{s>0}+\tfrac{2^{-ms}-2^{s}}{1-2^{s}}{\mathbbm{1}}_{s<0}\right]\|\mu\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{s,\infty}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}},\ \ m\in{\mathbb{N}}.

(2.10)

For a function
f:{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}\to{\mathbb{R}}
, the first-order difference operator is defined by

\delta^{(1)}_{h}f(z):=\delta_{h}f(z):=f(z+h)-f(z),\ \ z,h\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}.

For
M\in{\mathbb{N}}
, the
M
-order difference operator is defined recursively by

\delta^{(M+1)}_{h}f(z)=\delta_{h}\circ\delta^{(M)}_{h}f(z).

The following characterization is well-known (cf.
[
85
]
and
[
45
, Theorem 2.7]
).

Proposition 2.5
.

For
s>0
,
q\in[1,\infty]
and
{\boldsymbol{p}}\in[1,\infty]^{2}
, an equivalent norm of
{\mathbf{B}}^{s,q}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}
is given by

\displaystyle\|f\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{s,q}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\asymp\left(\int_{|h|_{\boldsymbol{a}}\leqslant 1}\left(\frac{\|\delta^{([s]+1)}f\|_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}{|h|^{s}_{\boldsymbol{a}}}\right)^{q}\frac{{\mathord{{\rm d}}}h}{|h|^{(2+\alpha)d}_{\boldsymbol{a}}}\right)^{1/q}+\|f\|_{{\boldsymbol{p}}},

(2.11)

where
[s]
is the integer part of
s
. In particular,
{\mathbf{C}}^{s}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}:=\mathbf{B}^{s,\infty}_{\infty;{\boldsymbol{a}}}
is the anisotropic
Hölder-Zygmund space, and for
s\in(0,1)
, there is a constant
C=C(\alpha,d,s)>0
such that

\|f\|_{{\mathbf{C}}^{s}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\asymp_{C}\|f\|_{\infty}+\sup_{z\not=z^{\prime}}|f(z)-f(z^{\prime})|/|z-z^{\prime}|^{s}_{\boldsymbol{a}}.

Similarly, the mixed and
x
-direction Hölder-Zygmund spaces are defined by

{\mathbf{C}}^{s_{0},s_{1}}_{x,{\boldsymbol{a}}}:={\mathbf{B}}^{s_{0},s_{1}}_{\infty;x,{\boldsymbol{a}}},\ \ {\mathbf{C}}^{s}_{x}:={\mathbf{B}}^{s}_{\infty;x}.

The following lemma provides important properties about Besov spaces. Although the proofs are standard,
for the readers’ convenience, we provide detailed proofs in the appendix.

Lemma 2.6
.

(i)

For any
{\boldsymbol{p}}\in[1,\infty]^{2}
,
s^{\prime}>s
and
q\in[1,\infty]
, it holds that

\displaystyle{\mathbf{B}}^{0,1}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{L}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}\hookrightarrow{\mathbf{B}}^{0,\infty}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}},\ \ {\mathbf{B}}^{s^{\prime},\infty}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}\hookrightarrow{\mathbf{B}}^{s,1}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}\hookrightarrow{\mathbf{B}}^{s,q}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}.

(2.12)

(ii)

For any
\beta,\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
q,q_{1},q_{2}\in[1,\infty]
and
{\boldsymbol{p}},{\boldsymbol{p}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{p}}_{2}\in[1,\infty]^{2}
with

\beta=\beta_{1}+\beta_{2},\ \ 1+\tfrac{1}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}=\tfrac{1}{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{1}}+\tfrac{1}{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{2}},\ \ \tfrac{1}{q}=\tfrac{1}{q_{1}}+\tfrac{1}{q_{2}},

it holds that

\displaystyle\|f*g\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta,q}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\leqslant 5\|f\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta_{1},q_{1}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{1};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\|g\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta_{2},q_{2}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{2};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}.

(2.13)

(iii)

For any
s>0
,
q\in[1,\infty]
and
{\boldsymbol{p}},{\boldsymbol{p}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{p}}_{2}\in[1,\infty]^{2}
with
\tfrac{1}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}=\tfrac{1}{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{1}}+\tfrac{1}{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{2}}
,
there is a constant
C=C(\alpha,d,s,q,{\boldsymbol{p}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{p}}_{2})>0
such that for any
(f,g)\in{\mathbf{B}}^{s,q}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{1};{\boldsymbol{a}}}\times{\mathbf{B}}^{s,q}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{2};{\boldsymbol{a}}}
,

\displaystyle\|fg\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{s,q}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\lesssim_{C}\|f\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{s,q}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{1};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\|g\|_{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{2}}+\|f\|_{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{1}}\|g\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{s,q}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{2};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}.

(2.14)

(iv)

Let
{\boldsymbol{p}},{\boldsymbol{p}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{p}}_{2}\in[1,\infty]^{2}
and
s,s_{0},s_{1}\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
\theta\in[0,1]
,
q,q_{1},q_{2}\in[1,\infty]
.
Suppose that

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{q}=\tfrac{1-\theta}{q_{1}}+\tfrac{\theta}{q_{2}},\ \tfrac{1}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\leqslant\tfrac{1-\theta}{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{1}}+\tfrac{\theta}{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{2}},\ \ s-{\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot\tfrac{d}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}=(1-\theta)\big(s_{1}-{\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot\tfrac{d}{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{1}}\big)+\theta\big(s_{2}-{\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot\tfrac{d}{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{2}}\big).

(2.15)

Then there is a constant
C=C({\boldsymbol{p}},{\boldsymbol{p}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{p}}_{2},s,s_{1},s_{2},\theta)>0
such that

\displaystyle\|f\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{s,q}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\lesssim_{C}\|f\|^{1-\theta}_{{\mathbf{B}}^{s_{1},q_{1}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{1};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\|f\|^{\theta}_{{\mathbf{B}}^{s_{2},q_{2}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{2};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}.

(2.16)

In particular, for
\boldsymbol{1}\leqslant{\boldsymbol{p}}_{1}\leqslant{\boldsymbol{p}}\leqslant\infty
,
q\in[1,\infty]
and
s=s_{1}+{\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot\big(\tfrac{d}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}-\tfrac{d}{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{1}}\big)
,

\displaystyle\|f\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{s,q}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\lesssim_{C}\|f\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{s_{1},q}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{1};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}.

(2.17)

(v)

For any
\varepsilon>0
and
{\boldsymbol{p}}\in[1,\infty]^{2}
,
(s_{0},s_{1})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
, we have the following embeddings:

\displaystyle{\mathbf{B}}^{{s_{0}-\varepsilon,s_{1}+\varepsilon}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};x,{\boldsymbol{a}}}\hookrightarrow{\mathbf{B}}^{{s_{0},s_{1}}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};x,{\boldsymbol{a}}}\hookrightarrow{\mathbf{B}}^{{s_{0}+\varepsilon,s_{1}-\varepsilon}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};x,{\boldsymbol{a}}},

(2.18)

and for
s_{1}>0
,

\displaystyle{\mathbf{B}}^{{s_{0},s_{1}}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};x,{\boldsymbol{a}}}\hookrightarrow{\mathbf{B}}^{{s_{0}+s_{1}}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};x}.

(2.19)

(vi)

For
\theta\in[0,1]
,
\gamma,\beta\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
{\boldsymbol{p}}\in[1,\infty]^{2}
, there is a constant
C=C(d,\theta,{\boldsymbol{p}})>0

such that

\displaystyle\|f\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\theta\gamma,(1-\theta)\beta}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};x,{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\lesssim\|f\|^{\theta}_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\gamma/(1+\alpha)}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};x}}\|f\|^{1-\theta}_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}.

(2.20)

Remark 2.7
.

Inequality (
2.16
) is a version of the classical Gagliardo-Nirenberg inequality in Besov spaces.
Inequality (
2.17
) is the classical Sobolev inequality.

2.2.
Time-weighted Schauder estimates

In this subsection we prove the basic time-weighted Schauder estimates for the model kinetic equation:

\partial_{t}u=(\Delta_{v}-v\cdot\nabla_{x})u+f
. For this goal, we need to make a detailed study for the kinetic semigroup.
Here much attention is paid on the large time estimate of kinetic semigroup in Besov spaces.

For
\alpha\in(0,2]
,
let
p_{t}(z)=p_{t}(x,v)
be the distributional density of
Z_{t}:=(\int_{0}^{t}L^{(\alpha)}_{s}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}s,L^{(\alpha)}_{t})
, where
L^{(\alpha)}_{t}
is the standard
\alpha
-stable process as in the introduction. Since

(L^{(\alpha)}_{\lambda t})_{t\geqslant 0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{=}}\lambda^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}(L^{(\alpha)}_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}
for
\lambda>0
,
it is easy to see that
p_{t}(x,v)

enjoys the following scaling property

\displaystyle p_{t}(x,v)=t^{-\frac{(2+\alpha)d}{\alpha}}p_{1}(t^{-\frac{1+\alpha}{\alpha}}x,t^{-\frac{1}{\alpha}}v),\ \ t>0.

(2.21)

The kinetic semigroup of operator
\Delta^{\alpha/2}_{v}-v\cdot\nabla_{x}
is given by

\displaystyle P_{t}f(z):={\mathbb{E}}f(\Gamma_{t}z+Z_{t})=\Gamma_{t}(p_{t}*f)(z)=(\Gamma_{t}p_{t}*\Gamma_{t}f)(z)=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\Gamma_{t}{p_{t}}(z-z^{\prime})\Gamma_{t}{f}(z^{\prime}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}z^{\prime},

(2.22)

where

\Gamma_{t}f(z):=f(\Gamma_{t}z):=f(x-tv,v).

The following lemma is originally proven in
[
44
]
(see also
[
85
]
).

Lemma 2.8
.

For any
l\geqslant 0
, there is a constant
C=C(l,\alpha,d)>0
such that

\|{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}p_{t}\|_{1}+\|{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}\Gamma_{t}p_{t}\|_{1}\lesssim_{C}(t2^{j\alpha})^{-l}\wedge 1,\ \forall j\geqslant 1,t>0.

Remark 2.9
.

The above estimate only holds for high frequency operator
{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}
,
j\geqslant 1
.

As an easy consequence we have

Lemma 2.10
.

For any
{\boldsymbol{p}}\in[1,\infty]^{2}
,
k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
and
\beta\in{\mathbb{R}}
, there is a constant
C>0
such that

\|\nabla^{k}_{v}p_{t}\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\lesssim_{C}t^{\frac{{\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot(d/{\boldsymbol{p}}-{\boldsymbol{d}})-k}{\alpha}}(1+t^{-\frac{\beta\vee 0}{\alpha}}),\ t>0.

Proof.

By scaling (
2.21
) and the change of variables, it is easy to see that for any
m\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
,

\displaystyle\|\nabla^{m}_{v}p_{t}\|_{\boldsymbol{p}}=t^{\frac{{\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot(d/{\boldsymbol{p}}-{\boldsymbol{d}})-m}{\alpha}}\|\nabla^{m}_{v}p_{1}\|_{\boldsymbol{p}}\lesssim_{C}t^{\frac{{\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot(d/{\boldsymbol{p}}-{\boldsymbol{d}})-m}{\alpha}}.

(2.23)

Without loss of generality, we assume
\beta\geqslant 0
. By definition, Bernstein’s inequality (
2.7
) and
Lemma
2.8
with
l=\frac{\beta+k+{\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot({\boldsymbol{d}}-d/{\boldsymbol{p}})}{\alpha}
, we have

\displaystyle\|\nabla^{k}_{v}p_{t}\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\|{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{0}\nabla^{k}_{v}p_{t}\|_{\boldsymbol{p}}+\sup_{j\geqslant 1}\big(2^{j\beta}\|{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}\nabla^{k}_{v}p_{t}\|_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\big)

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\nabla^{k}_{v}p_{t}\|_{\boldsymbol{p}}+\sup_{j\geqslant 1}2^{j(\beta+k+{\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot({\boldsymbol{d}}-d/{\boldsymbol{p}}))}\|{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}p_{t}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\lesssim t^{\frac{{\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot(d/{\boldsymbol{p}}-{\boldsymbol{d}})-k}{\alpha}}+t^{\frac{{\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot(d/{\boldsymbol{p}}-{\boldsymbol{d}})-k-\beta}{\alpha}}.

The proof is complete.
∎

The following lemma is proved in
[
44
, Lemma 6.7]
. For the readers’ convenience, we reproduce the proof here.

Lemma 2.11
.

For
t>0
and
j\geqslant 1
, we define

\Theta^{t}_{j}:=\Big\{\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}\,\big|\,2^{\ell}\leqslant 2^{\alpha+2}(2^{j}+t2^{j(1+\alpha)}),\ 2^{j}\leqslant 2^{\alpha+2}(2^{\ell}+t2^{\ell(1+\alpha)})\Big\}.

(i)

For any
\ell\notin\Theta^{t}_{j}
, it holds that

\langle{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}f,\Gamma_{t}{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{\ell}g\rangle=0.

(ii)

For any
0\not=\beta\in{\mathbb{R}}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in\Theta^{t}_{j}}2^{-\ell\beta}\lesssim 2^{-j\beta}(1+(t2^{j\alpha}))^{|\beta|}.

(2.24)

Proof.

(i) By Parsavel’s identity of Fourier’s transform, we have

\displaystyle\langle{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}f,\Gamma_{t}{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{\ell}g\rangle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}\widehat{{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}f}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\widehat{\Gamma_{t}{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{\ell}g}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}\xi_{1}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}\xi_{2}

\displaystyle\qquad=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}\phi^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})f(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\phi^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{\ell}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}-t\xi_{1})g(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}-t\xi_{1}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}\xi_{1}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}\xi_{2}.

Note that

{\rm supp}\phi^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}\subset\{\xi:2^{j-1}\leqslant|\xi|_{\boldsymbol{a}}\leqslant 2^{j+1}\}.

For fixed
j\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
t>0
, it is easy to see that
\langle{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}f,\Gamma_{t}{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{\ell}g\rangle=0
if and only if

\{\xi:2^{j-1}\leqslant|\xi|_{\boldsymbol{a}}\leqslant 2^{j+1}\}\cap\{\xi=(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}):2^{\ell-1}\leqslant|(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}-t\xi_{1})|_{\boldsymbol{a}}\leqslant 2^{\ell+1}\}=\emptyset,

which is equivalent to say
\ell\notin\Theta^{t}_{j}
.

(ii) Fix
\beta>0
. Note that for
\ell\in\Theta^{t}_{j}
with
\ell\leqslant j
,

2^{-\ell}\leqslant 2^{\alpha+2}2^{-j}(1+t2^{\ell\alpha})\leqslant 2^{\alpha+2}2^{-j}(1+t2^{j\alpha})=:D\Rightarrow\ell\geqslant-\ln D/\ln 2.

Hence,

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in\Theta^{t}_{j}}2^{-\ell\beta}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell\in\Theta^{t}_{j};\ell\geqslant j}2^{-\ell\beta}+\sum_{\ell\in\Theta^{t}_{j};\ell<j}2^{-\ell\beta}\leqslant\sum_{\ell\geqslant j}2^{-\ell\beta}+\sum_{-\ln D/\ln 2\leqslant\ell}2^{-\ell\beta}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{2^{-j\beta}}{1-2^{-\beta}}+\frac{D^{\beta}}{1-2^{-\beta}}\lesssim 2^{-j\beta}(1+t2^{j\alpha})^{\beta}.

Similarly, for
D:=2^{\alpha+2}2^{j}(1+t2^{j\alpha})
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in\Theta^{t}_{j}}2^{\ell\beta}
