p\in[2,\infty]
and
{\boldsymbol{p}}:=(p,1)
,

\displaystyle\sup_{t\geqslant 1}\left(t^{\frac{d(1+\alpha)}{\alpha}\cdot(1-\frac{1}{p})}\|f(t)\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta,1}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\right)<\infty.

(5.5)

In particular,

\displaystyle\sup_{t\geqslant 1}\left(t^{\frac{(\alpha+1)(d-1)}{\alpha}}\|E(t)\|_{\infty}\right)<\infty.

(5.6)

Proof.

To apply Theorem
3.14
, we need to first choose suitable parameters so that
(H
0
)
holds.
Let

\boldsymbol{\varrho}_{0}=(1,\infty),\boldsymbol{\varrho}_{1}=(2,\infty),q_{b}=\infty,\ \beta_{b}=1+\alpha,\ {\boldsymbol{p}}_{1}:=(2,1).

With these choice of parameters, it is easy to see that

{\mathscr{A}}_{0}:={\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot(\tfrac{d}{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{0}}+\tfrac{d}{\boldsymbol{\varrho}_{0}}-{\boldsymbol{d}})=\tfrac{(1+\alpha)d}{p_{0}},\ \ {\mathscr{A}}_{1}:={\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot(\tfrac{d}{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{1}}+\tfrac{d}{\boldsymbol{\varrho}_{1}}-{\boldsymbol{d}})=0.

By (
5.2
), one sees that
\beta_{b}\not={\mathscr{A}}_{i}-\beta_{0}
,
i=0,1
and

0<{\mathscr{A}}_{0}-\beta_{0}-\beta_{b}\leqslant\alpha-1,\ \ {\mathscr{A}}_{0}-\beta_{0}-\beta_{b}<\alpha+\beta_{0},

which means that
(H
0
)
holds.
Moreover,
{\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot\tfrac{d}{\boldsymbol{\varrho}_{1}}=\tfrac{(1+\alpha)d}{2}>\alpha
. Thus by Lemma
5.1
and
Theorem
3.14
, there is a constant
C_{0}>0

depending on
\|f_{0}\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta_{0}}_{\boldsymbol{1};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}
such that if

\displaystyle\|f_{0}\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta_{0}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{0};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}+\|f_{0}\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta_{0}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{1};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\leqslant C_{0},

(5.7)

then there is a unique smooth solution
f
so that (
5.4
) and
(
5.5
) for
{\boldsymbol{p}}={\boldsymbol{p}}_{0},{\boldsymbol{p}}_{1}
hold.
Let
\theta\in[0,1]
be defined by
\frac{1-\theta}{p_{0}}+\theta=\frac{1}{2}
.
Note that by the interpolation inequality (
2.16
),

\|f_{0}\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta_{0}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{1};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\lesssim\|f_{0}\|^{1-\theta}_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta_{0}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{0};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\|f_{0}\|^{\theta}_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta_{0}}_{\boldsymbol{1};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}.

By adjusting the small constant
C_{0}
in (
5.3
), one sees that (
5.3
) implies (
5.7
).

Now we devote to showing (
5.5
) for all
{\boldsymbol{p}}=(p,1)
, where
p\in[2,\infty]
.
If
p_{0}=\infty
, then by the interpolation inequality (
2.16
), we immediately have

\displaystyle\|f(t)\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta,1}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\leqslant\|f(t)\|^{\frac{2}{p}}_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta,1}_{(2,1);{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\|f(t)\|^{1-\frac{2}{p}}_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta,1}_{(\infty,1);{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\lesssim t^{-\frac{d(1+\alpha)}{\alpha}\cdot(1-\frac{1}{p})},\ \ t\geqslant 1.

(5.8)

If
p_{0}<\infty
, then for
\beta>\frac{d}{p_{0}}
, by the Sobolev embedding (
2.17
) and (
5.5
) for
{\boldsymbol{p}}={\boldsymbol{p}}_{0}
, we have

\|f(t)\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta}_{(\infty,1);{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\lesssim\|f(t)\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta,1}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{0};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\lesssim t^{-\frac{d(1+\alpha)}{\alpha}\cdot(1-\frac{1}{p_{0}})},\ \ t\geqslant 1.

In particular, one can choose
t_{0}\geqslant 1
large enough so that

\|f(t_{0})\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta}_{(\infty,1);{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\leqslant C_{0}.

Starting from the time
t_{0}
and with the choice of
p_{0}=\infty
, we then deduce (
5.5
) by (
5.8
).

Finally, for (
5.6
), by (ii) of Lemma
A.3
with
\gamma=d-1
and
p=1
, we have
for
t\geqslant 1
,

\displaystyle\|E(t)\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\|\nabla U*\langle f\rangle(t)\|_{\infty}\lesssim\|\langle f\rangle(t)\|_{1}^{\frac{1}{d}}\|\langle f\rangle(t)\|_{\infty}^{\frac{d-1}{d}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|f(t)\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{1}}^{\frac{1}{d}}\|f(t)\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{0,1}_{(\infty,1);{\boldsymbol{a}}}}^{\frac{d-1}{d}}\lesssim t^{-\frac{(\alpha+1)(d-1)}{\alpha}}.

The proof is complete.
∎

Remark 5.3
.

Theorem
5.2
extends the well-posedness theory for VPFP equations to the distribution initial data
(see
[
77
]
,
[
10
]
,
[
14
]
,
[
64
]
).
Notice that when
\alpha=2
, in three dimensional case, Carrillo and Soler
[
14
]
studied the well-posedness of VPFP equation
with initial data being in Morrey spaces.
More precisely, under more restricted smallness assumption on initial data,

\|f_{0}\|_{{\mathcal{M}}}+\|f_{0}\|_{M\widetilde{L}_{9/4}}\leqslant\varepsilon,

where
M\widetilde{L}_{p}
is some Morrey space (see
[
14
, (1.4)]
), they showed global well-posedess and obtained the following decay estimate for the force field
E(t,x)
,

\|E(t)\|_{\infty}\lesssim t^{-1/2},\ \ t\geqslant 1.

Note that one can check that for any
p_{0}\in(9/4,3)
,

M\widetilde{L}_{9/4}\cap{\mathcal{M}}\subset{\mathbf{B}}^{0}_{(9/4,1);{\boldsymbol{a}}}\cap{\mathbf{B}}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{1};{\boldsymbol{a}}}\subset{\mathbf{B}}^{9/p_{0}-4}_{(p_{0},1);{\boldsymbol{a}}}\cap{\mathbf{B}}^{9/p_{0}-4}_{\boldsymbol{1};{\boldsymbol{a}}}.

Later, under smallness condition
\|f_{0}\|_{L^{1}}+\|f_{0}\|_{L^{1}_{v}(L^{\infty}_{x})}\leqslant\varepsilon
,
Ono and Strauss
[
64
]
showed the following optimal rate of large time decay

\displaystyle\|E(t)\|_{\infty}\lesssim t^{-3(d-1)/2},\ \ t\geqslant 1,

which coincides with (
5.6
).
It is pointed out that requiring small
\|f_{0}\|_{{\mathcal{M}}}
rules out the application for the associated mean-field SDE since we always have

\|f_{0}\|_{{\mathcal{M}}}\equiv 1
for SDEs. In fact, we have paid much efforts to drop the smallness of
\|f_{0}\|_{{\mathcal{M}}}
in Theorem
3.14
.

Now we consider the following mean-field SDE:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &X_{t}=X_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}V_{s}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}s,\\
&V_{t}=V_{0}+\int^{t}_{0}(K*\mu_{X_{s}})(X_{s}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}s+L^{(\alpha)}_{t}.\end{aligned}\right.

(5.9)

By Theorem
1.3
we have

Theorem 5.4
.

Let
\alpha\in(1,2]
,
\beta_{0}\in(-\alpha,0)
,
p_{0}\in[2,\infty]
,
{\boldsymbol{p}}_{0}:=(p_{0},1)
and
(X_{0},V_{0})\sim\mu_{0}\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d})
.

(A)

Suppose that

\tfrac{(1+\alpha)d}{p_{0}}<2\alpha+1+\beta_{0}+\beta_{0}\wedge(-1).

Then there is a constant
C_{0}=C_{0}(\alpha,\beta_{0},p_{0},d)>0
such that if

\displaystyle\|\mu_{0}\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta_{0}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{0};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\leqslant C_{0},

(5.10)

then there is a unique weak solution
Z_{t}=(X_{t},V_{t})
to SDE (
5.9
) such that
for each
t>0
,
Z_{t}
admits a smooth density
f(t)
that satisfies (
5.4
) and (
5.5
).

(B)

Suppose that

\displaystyle\tfrac{(1+\alpha)d}{p_{0}}<\tfrac{5}{2}\alpha-2+\beta_{0}.

Then under (
5.10
), there is a unique strong solution to SDE (
5.9
).

Moreover, when
d=3
and
\alpha=2
, and if
{\mathbb{E}}|V_{0}|^{2}<\infty
, then
the above
(A)
and
(B)
still hold without smallness condition (
5.10
).

Proof.

Note that by (
2.9
),

\|\mu_{0}\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{1};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\leqslant C(d,\alpha).

As the choice of parameters in Theorem
5.2
,

(A)
and
(B)
are direct applications of Theorem
1.3
.
When
d=3
and
\alpha=2
, in order to obtain a global solution without smallness condition (
5.10
),
we shall use a result of
[
64
, Theorem 5.1]
. More precisely, it suffices to find a small time
t_{0}>0
so that

\|f(t_{0})\|_{L^{1}_{v}(L^{\infty}_{x})}:=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}|f(t_{0},x,v)|{\mathord{{\rm d}}}v<\infty

and

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}|v|^{2}f(t_{0},x,v){\mathord{{\rm d}}}x{\mathord{{\rm d}}}v<\infty.

For any
f_{0}\in{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta_{0}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{0};{\boldsymbol{a}}}
, by Theorem
3.17
,
one can find a
t_{0}>0
small enough so that
there is a local solution for SDE (
5.9
) up to time
t_{0}
. Moreover, for any
\beta\geqslant 0
,

\sup_{t\in(0,t_{0}]}\left(t^{\beta/\alpha}\|f(t)\|_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{\beta+\beta_{0}}}\right)<\infty.

By (
5.9
) we have

\displaystyle({\mathbb{E}}|V_{t}|^{2})^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leqslant({\mathbb{E}}|V_{0}|^{2})^{1/2}+\left({\mathbb{E}}\Big|\int_{0}^{t}(K*\mu_{X_{s}})(X_{s}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}s\Big|^{2}\right)^{1/2}+\sqrt{2}({\mathbb{E}}|W_{t}|^{2})^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leqslant({\mathbb{E}}|V_{0}|^{2})^{1/2}+\int_{0}^{t}\|K*\mu_{X_{s}}\|_{\infty}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}s+\sqrt{2t},

which implies by (i) of Lemma
4.7
that for any
t\in[0,t_{0}]
,

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}|v|^{2}f(t,x,v){\mathord{{\rm d}}}x{\mathord{{\rm d}}}v={\mathbb{E}}|V_{t}|^{2}<\infty.

By (
2.12
) and (
2.17
), for any
t\in(0,t_{0}]
and
\beta>3(1+\alpha)(1-\frac{1}{p_{0}})
, we have

\|f(t)\|_{L^{1}_{v}(L^{\infty}_{x})}\leqslant\sum_{j\geqslant 0}\|{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}f(t)\|_{L^{1}_{v}(L^{\infty}_{x})}=\|f(t)\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{0,1}_{(\infty,1);{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\lesssim\|f(t)\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{0};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}<\infty,

and for
\beta>3(1+\alpha)(1-\frac{1}{p_{0}})+3
,

\|f(t)\|_{L^{\infty}_{x,v}}\lesssim\|f(t)\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{0};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}<\infty.

Now starting from time
t_{0}/2
, we can use
[
64
, Theorem 5.1]
to find a unique global smooth solution
\widetilde{f}
for (
5.1
) with initial value
\widetilde{f}(0)=f(t_{0}/2)
. Since
f
is smooth on
[\frac{t_{0}}{2},t_{0}]
, by the uniqueness we have
f(t+t_{0}/2)=\widetilde{f}(t)
on
[0,\frac{t_{0}}{2}]
.
Thus we can patch up the solutions and conclude the proof.
∎

Remark 5.5
.

For
\alpha\in(1,2)
, it seems to be an open question to obtain the global solution for fractional VPFP equation
since there is no finite second-order moment for
\alpha
-stable process.

6.
Application to fractional Navier-Stokes equations and SQG equations

In this section we apply our results to the 2D and 3D fractional Navier-Stokes equations:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u=\Delta^{\alpha/2}u+u\cdot\nabla u+\nabla p,\ \ \mathord{{\rm div}}u=0,

(6.1)

where
\alpha\in(1,2]
,
u
is the velocity field and
p
is the pressure.
For 2D fractional Navier-Stokes equations, we show the global well-posedness
when the initial vorticity is in
{\mathbf{B}}^{-\frac{1+\alpha}{2}+}_{\infty}:=\cup_{\varepsilon>0}{\mathbf{B}}^{\varepsilon-\frac{1+\alpha}{2}}_{\infty}
.
For 3D fractional Navier-Stokes equations, we show the global well-posedness when the
initial vorticity is in
{\mathbf{B}}^{-\frac{1+\alpha}{2}+}_{\infty}
and small.
To the best of our knowledge, these results are new for fractional Navier-Stokes equations.
Moreover, we also consider the SQG equation in Example 2 in the introduction.

We first consider the 2D fractional NSEs with initial vorticity in Besov space
{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta_{0}}_{p_{0}}
.
Let
w={\rm curl}(u)
be the vorticity of
u
that satisfies the following scalar equation in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}w=\Delta^{\alpha/2}w+u\cdot\nabla w,

(6.2)

where the velocity field
u
can be recovered from
w
by the Biot-Savart law (cf.
[
60
]
):

u(x)=K_{2}*w(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\frac{(y_{2}-x_{2},x_{1}-y_{1})}{|x-y|^{2}}w(y){\mathord{{\rm d}}}y.

By (iv) of Lemma
A.3
, one has

\displaystyle K_{2}\in{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta}_{\varrho_{0}}+{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta}_{\varrho_{1}},\ \ \beta=\tfrac{2}{\varrho_{0}}-1,\ \varrho_{0}\in[1,2),\ \varrho_{1}\in(2,\infty].

(6.3)

By Theorem
3.22
we have

Theorem 6.1
.

Let
\alpha\in(1,2]
,
p_{0}\in[1,\infty]
and
\beta_{0}\in(-\frac{\alpha+1}{2},0)
satisfy

\displaystyle\tfrac{2}{p_{0}}\leqslant\alpha+\beta_{0},\ \beta_{0}\in(-1,0),\ \ \tfrac{2}{p_{0}}<\alpha+1+2\beta_{0},\ \beta_{0}\in(-\tfrac{\alpha+1}{2},-1].

(6.4)

For any
w_{0}\in\cup_{q\in[1,\infty)}({\mathbf{B}}^{\beta_{0},q}_{p_{0}}\cap{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta_{0},q}_{1})
, there is a unique global smooth solution
w
to vorticity equation (
6.2
) on
{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
so that for any
T>0
and
\beta\geqslant 0
,

\sup_{t\in(0,T]}\left(t^{-(\beta-\beta_{0})/\alpha}\big(\|w(t)\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta,1}_{p_{0}}}+\|w(t)\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta,1}_{1}}\big)\right)<\infty.

Proof.

We only need to check that under (
6.4
), the assumptions in Theorem
3.22
hold for
b=K_{2}
and
q_{b}=\infty
.
Let
\varrho_{0}=1
,
\varrho_{1}=\infty
and
\beta_{b}=\tfrac{2}{\varrho_{0}}-1=1
. By (
6.3
), one sees that the assumptions of Theorem
3.22
hold. Moreover, since
\mathord{{\rm div}}K_{2}=0
, we have the global solution.
∎

Remark 6.2
.

If we take
p_{0}=\infty
in (
6.4
), then for any
w_{0}\in{\mathbf{B}}^{-\frac{1+\alpha}{2}+}_{\infty}\cap{\mathbf{B}}^{-\frac{1+\alpha}{2}+}_{1}
, there is
a unique global smooth solution to equation (
6.2
) on
{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
. Since the two dimensional Brownian white noise locally
belongs to
{\mathbf{B}}^{-1-}_{\infty}
, one can apply Theorem
6.1
to the 2D fractional Navier-Stokes equations with initial vorticity being the Brownian white noise.

Now we consider the following mean-field SDE in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
with kernel
K_{2}
:

\displaystyle{\mathord{{\rm d}}}X_{t}=(K_{2}*\mu_{X_{t}})(X_{t}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}t+{\mathord{{\rm d}}}L^{(\alpha)}_{t},\ \ X_{0}\sim\mu_{0}\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{R}}^{2}).

(6.5)

By Theorem
1.9
with
\varrho_{1}=\infty
, we have

Theorem 6.3
.

Let
\alpha\in(1,2]
,
p_{0}\in[1,\infty]
and
\beta_{0}\in(-\frac{\alpha+1}{2},0)
satisfy

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\tfrac{2}{p_{0}}<\alpha+1+\beta_{0}+\beta_{0}\wedge(-1),&\ \ {\rm weak\ solution},\\
&\tfrac{2}{p_{0}}<\tfrac{3\alpha}{2}-1+\beta_{0},&\ \ {\rm strong\ solution}.\end{aligned}\right.

For any
\mu_{0}\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})\cap{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta_{0}}_{p_{0}}
, there exists a unique weak/strong solution
X_{t}
for SDE (
6.5
)
so that for each
t>0
,
X_{t}
has a smooth density
\mu_{X_{t}}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}x)=\rho_{t}(x){\mathord{{\rm d}}}x

with regularity that for any
T,\beta\geqslant 0
,

\sup_{t\in(0,T]}\left(t^{\frac{\beta}{\alpha}}\|\rho_{t}\|_{{\mathbf{B}}_{p_{0}}^{\beta+\beta_{0}}}\right)<\infty.

Next we consider the following mean-field SDE in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
associated with SQG (
1.16
):

\displaystyle{\mathord{{\rm d}}}X_{t}=({\mathscr{R}}*\mu_{X_{t}})(X_{t}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}t+{\mathord{{\rm d}}}L^{(\alpha)}_{t},\ \ X_{0}\sim\mu_{0}\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{R}}^{2}),

(6.6)

where
{\mathscr{R}}=(-x_{2},x_{1})/|x|^{3}
. Note that by (iii) of Lemma
A.3
,

{\mathscr{R}}\in{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta}_{\varrho_{0}},\ \ \beta=\tfrac{2}{\varrho_{0}}-2,\ \varrho_{0}\in(1,\infty].

By Theorem
1.9
with
\varrho_{1}=\varrho_{0}
, we similarly have

Theorem 6.4
.

Let
\alpha\in(1,2]
,
p_{0}\in[1,\infty]
and
\beta_{0}\in(-\alpha,0)
satisfy

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\tfrac{2}{p_{0}}<\alpha+\beta_{0}+\beta_{0}\wedge(-1),&\ \ {\rm weak\ solution},\\
&\tfrac{2}{p_{0}}<\tfrac{3\alpha}{2}-2+\beta_{0},&\ {\rm strong\ solution}.\end{aligned}\right.

For any
\mu_{0}\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})\cap{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta_{0}}_{p_{0}}
, there exists a unique weak/strong solution
X_{t}
for SDE (
6.6
)
so that for each
t>0
,
X_{t}
has a smooth density
\mu_{X_{t}}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}x)=\rho_{t}(x){\mathord{{\rm d}}}x
with regularity that for any
T,\beta\geqslant 0
,

\sup_{t\in(0,T]}\left(t^{\frac{\beta}{\alpha}}\|\rho_{t}\|_{{\mathbf{B}}_{p_{0}}^{\beta+\beta_{0}}}\right)<\infty.

Remark 6.5
.

From the condition, one sees that for strong solutions, we need to assume
\alpha\in(\tfrac{4}{3},2]
.

Finally we consider the 3D fractional NSEs with initial vorticity in non-homogeneous space
{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta_{0}}_{p_{0}}
.
Let
w={\rm curl}(u)
be the vorticity of
u
. Then

\displaystyle\partial_{t}w=\Delta^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}w-u\cdot\nabla w+w\cdot\nabla u=\Delta^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}w+\mathord{{\rm div}}(u\otimes w-w\otimes u),

(6.7)

where for two divergence free vector fields
w_{1},w_{2}
,

\mathord{{\rm div}}(w_{1}\otimes w_{2}):=w_{1}\cdot\nabla w_{2}.

By the Biot-Savart law, one knows that (cf.
[
60
]
)

u(x)=K_{3}*w(x)=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}K_{3}(x-y)w(y){\mathord{{\rm d}}}y,

where

K_{3}(x)h=\frac{1}{4\pi}\frac{x\times h}{|x|^{3}},\ h\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}.

Note that by (iv) of Lemma
A.3
,

\displaystyle K_{3}\in{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta}_{\varrho_{0}}+{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta}_{\varrho_{1}},\ \ \beta=\tfrac{3}{\varrho_{0}}-2,\ \varrho_{0}\in[1,\tfrac{3}{2}),\ \varrho_{1}\in(\tfrac{3}{2},\infty].

(6.8)

By Duhamel’s formula, we have

w(t)=P_{t}w_{0}+\int^{t}_{0}P_{t-s}\mathord{{\rm div}}((K_{3}*w)\otimes w-w\otimes(K_{3}*w)){\mathord{{\rm d}}}s.

The following result can be proved by using similar argument as in proving Theorem
3.14
.

Theorem 6.6
.

Let
\alpha\in(1,2]
,
p_{0}\in[2,\infty]
and
\beta_{0}\in(-\alpha,0)
satisfy

\displaystyle\tfrac{3}{p_{0}}\leqslant\alpha+\beta_{0},\ \beta_{0}\in(-1,0),\ \ \tfrac{3}{p_{0}}<\alpha+1+2\beta_{0},\ \beta_{0}\in(-\tfrac{\alpha+1}{2},-1].

(6.9)

For any initial vorticity
w_{0}\in{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta_{0}}_{p_{0}}\cap{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta_{0}}_{1}
,
there is a constant
C_{0}=C_{0}(\alpha,\beta_{0},p_{0},\|w_{0}\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta_{0}}_{1}})>0
such that if

\displaystyle\|w_{0}\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta_{0}}_{p_{0}}}\leqslant C_{0},

(6.10)

then there is a unique global smooth solution
w
to equation (
6.7
) on
{\mathbb{R}}_{+}

so that for any
\beta\geqslant 0
,

\displaystyle\sup_{t>0}\left(t^{(\beta-\beta_{0})/\alpha}\big(\|w(t)\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta,1}_{p_{0}}}+\|w(t)\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta,1}_{1}}\big)\right)<\infty

(6.11)

and for any
p\in[2,\infty]
and
\beta\geqslant 0
, there is a constant
C=C(\alpha,p,\beta,\beta_{0},p_{0},\|w_{0}\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta_{0}}_{1}})>0
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{t\geqslant 1}\left(t^{(3-\frac{3}{p})/\alpha}\|w(t)\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta,1}_{p}}\right)<\infty.

(6.12)

Proof.

For given
\beta_{0},p_{0}
satisfying (
6.9
), by (
6.8
),
one can choose
\varrho_{0}=1
,
\varrho_{1}=2
,
\beta_{b}=\tfrac{3}{\varrho_{0}}-2=1
,
q_{b}=\infty
so that

b=K_{3}\in{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta_{b}}_{\varrho_{0}}+{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta_{b}}_{\varrho_{1}}
, and (
3.61
) holds and

\tfrac{1}{p_{0}}+\tfrac{1}{\varrho_{0}}\geqslant 1,\ \ \ \tfrac{3}{\varrho_{1}}>\alpha-1.

Define

{\mathcal{H}}_{t}(u,w):=\int^{t}_{0}P_{t-s}\mathord{{\rm div}}((K_{3}*u)\otimes w-w\otimes(K_{3}*u)){\mathord{{\rm d}}}s.

As in the proof of Theorem
3.14
, one can show that there is a small constant
C_{0}>0
such that under condition (
6.10
), there is a unique global smooth solution
w
satisfying

w(t)=P_{t}w_{0}+{\mathcal{H}}_{t}(w,w),

and (
6.11
) and (
6.12
) for
p=2,p_{0}
hold.

Now we devote to showing (
6.12
) for all
p\in[2,\infty]
.
Fix
\beta\geqslant 0
. Note that it is completely the same as proving Theorem
3.14

that if
p_{0}=\infty
, then

\displaystyle\sup_{t\geqslant 1}\Big(t^{3/\alpha}\|w(t)\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta,1}_{\infty}}+t^{3/(2\alpha)}\|w(t)\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta,1}_{2}}\Big)<\infty,

which implies by interpolation inequality (
2.16
),

\displaystyle\|w(t)\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta,1}_{p}}\leqslant\|w(t)\|^{\frac{2}{p}}_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta,1}_{2}}\|w(t)\|^{1-\frac{2}{p}}_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta,1}_{\infty}}\lesssim t^{-(3-\frac{3}{p})/\alpha},\ \ t\geqslant 1.

(6.13)

If
p_{0}<\infty
, by (
6.11
) with
\beta=\frac{3}{p_{0}}
and the Sobolev embedding inequality (
2.17
), we have

\displaystyle\|w(t)\|_{\infty}\lesssim\|w(t)\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{3/p_{0},1}_{p_{0}}}\lesssim t^{-(3-3/p_{0})/\alpha},\ \ t\geqslant 1,

and

\sup_{t\geqslant 1}\|w(t)\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{0,1}_{1}}<\infty.

In particular, we can choose
t_{0}
large enough so that for the small constant
C_{0}
in (
6.10
),

\|w(t_{0})\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta_{0}}_{\infty;{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\leqslant C\|w(t_{0})\|_{\infty}\leqslant C_{0}.

Now starting from
t_{0}
, we can use (
6.13
) to conclude the proof of (
6.12
).
∎

Remark 6.7
.

When
\alpha=2
, it is by no means that Theorem
6.6
is new. For fractional Navier-Stokes equation with
\alpha\in(1,2)
,
Wu
[
80
, Theorem 6.1]
showed the global well-posedness for small initial velocity in homogeneous Besov spaces.
As one knows, there are vast literatures about 3D Navier-Stokes equations. Here we only want to emphasize that
we present a general framework that contains Navier-Stokes equations as one application.

7.
Application to fractional kinetic porous medium equation with viscosity

In this section we consider the following fractional kinetic porous medium equation with viscosity

\displaystyle\partial_{t}f=\Delta_{v}^{\alpha/2}f-v\cdot\nabla_{x}f+\mathord{{\rm div}}_{v}((\nabla_{v}\Delta^{-s}_{v}f)f),

(7.1)

where
s\in(0,1]
and
d\geqslant 3
. Note that

(\nabla_{v}\Delta^{-s}_{v}f)(x,v)=K*f(x,v)

with

K(x,v):=c_{d,s}\delta_{0}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}x)\nabla_{v}|v|^{2s-d},

where
c_{d,s}>0
is a normalized constant and
\delta_{0}
is the Dirac measure at point
0
.

The following lemma provides the necessary regularity estimate for
K
.

Lemma 7.1
.

Fix
p_{0}\in[1,\infty]
. Let
{\boldsymbol{p}}=(p_{x},p_{v})
with
p_{x}\in[1,\infty]
and
p_{v}\in[p_{0},\infty]
.
For any
\beta_{0}<\frac{d}{p_{0}}-\frac{d}{p_{v}}
, there is a constant
C=C(\alpha,d,p_{0},\beta_{0},{\boldsymbol{p}})>0
such that for any

U(v)\in{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta_{0}}_{p_{0}}
,

\displaystyle\|\delta_{0}\otimes U\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta_{0}+d-\frac{d}{p_{0}}-{\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot({\boldsymbol{d}}-\frac{d}{{\boldsymbol{p}}})}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\lesssim_{C}\|U\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta_{0}}_{p_{0}}}.

(7.2)

In particular, for
s\in(0,1]
and
\boldsymbol{\varrho}_{i}=(1,\varrho_{i})
,
i=0,1
, where
\varrho_{0}\in[1,\infty]
and
\varrho_{1}\in(\frac{d}{d-2s},\infty]
,

\displaystyle\delta_{0}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}x)\nabla|v|^{2s-d}\in{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta_{b}}_{\boldsymbol{\varrho}_{0};{\boldsymbol{a}}}+{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta_{b}}_{\boldsymbol{\varrho}_{1};{\boldsymbol{a}}},

(7.3)

where
\beta_{b}<(2s-1-d+\frac{d}{\varrho_{0}})\wedge 0
.

Proof.

By definition (
2.3
) we have

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}(\delta_{0}\otimes U)(x,v)=(\check{\phi}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}*_{v}U)(x,v)=\sum_{k\geqslant 0}(\check{\phi}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}*_{v}{\mathcal{R}}^{v}_{k}U)(x,v),

where
*_{v}
stands for the convolution with respect to the variable
v
and
{\mathcal{R}}^{v}_{k}U=\check{\phi}_{k}*U
.
Noting that

\widehat{(\check{\phi}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}*_{v}{\mathcal{R}}^{v}_{k}U)}(\xi,\eta)=\phi^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}(\xi,\eta)\phi_{k}(\eta)\hat{U}(\eta),

and by (
2.1
) and (
2.6
),

\phi^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}(\xi,\eta)\phi_{k}(\eta)=0,\ \ k>j+2,

by Young’s inequality, scaling of
\check{\phi}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}
and Bernstein’s inequality (
2.7
), we have for any
j\geqslant 0
,

\displaystyle\|{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}(\delta_{0}\otimes U)\|_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{k\leqslant j+2}\|\check{\phi}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}*_{v}{\mathcal{R}}^{v}_{k}U\|_{(p_{x},p_{v})}\leqslant\sum_{k\leqslant j+2}\|\check{\phi}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}\|_{(p_{x},1)}\|{\mathcal{R}}^{v}_{k}U\|_{p_{v}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\leqslant j+2}2^{j(1+\alpha)(d-\frac{d}{p_{x}})}\|\check{\phi}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{1}\|_{(p_{x},1)}\|{\mathcal{R}}^{v}_{k}U\|_{p_{v}}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{j(1+\alpha)(d-\frac{d}{p_{x}})}\sum_{k\leqslant j+2}2^{k(\frac{d}{p}-\frac{d}{p_{v}}-\beta)}\|U\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta_{0}}_{p_{0}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{j({\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot({\boldsymbol{d}}-\frac{d}{{\boldsymbol{p}}})+\frac{d}{p_{0}}-d-\beta_{0})}\|U\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta_{0}}_{p_{0}}},

where the last step is due to
\beta_{0}<\frac{d}{p_{0}}-\frac{d}{p_{v}}
.
Thus we get (
7.2
).
Now, fix
s\in(0,1]
.
Let
U(v):=\nabla_{v}|v|^{2s-d}
. For any

\varrho_{1}\in(\frac{d}{d-2s},\infty]
, by (iv) of Lemma
A.3
, we have

U\in{\mathbf{B}}^{2s-1}_{1}+{\mathbf{B}}^{2s-1}_{\varrho_{1}}.

Now for any
\varrho_{0}\in[1,\infty]
,
if we take
\beta_{0}<(2s-1)\wedge(d-\frac{d}{\varrho_{0}})
,
p_{0}=1
and
{\boldsymbol{p}}=(1,\varrho_{0})
in (
7.2
),
then for
U_{0}\in{\mathbf{B}}^{2s-1}_{1}\subset{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta_{0}}_{1}
,

\delta_{0}\otimes U_{0}\in{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta_{0}+d/\varrho_{0}-d}_{\boldsymbol{\varrho}_{0};{\boldsymbol{a}}}.

Similarly, for any
\varrho_{1}\in(\frac{d}{d-2s},\infty]
, if we take
\beta_{0}=\beta_{b}<0
,

p_{0}=\varrho_{1}
and
{\boldsymbol{p}}=(1,\varrho_{1})
in (
7.2
),
then for
U_{1}\in{\mathbf{B}}^{2s-1}_{\varrho_{1}}\subset{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta_{b}}_{\varrho_{1}}
,

\delta_{0}\otimes U_{1}\in{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta_{b}}_{\boldsymbol{\varrho}_{1};{\boldsymbol{a}}}.

Thus we obtain (
7.3
) by noting
\beta_{0}+\frac{d}{\varrho_{0}}-d<(2s-1)\wedge(d-\frac{d}{\varrho_{0}})+\frac{d}{\varrho_{0}}-d=(2s-1-d+\frac{d}{\varrho_{0}})\wedge 0
.
∎

The associated density-distribution dependent SDE to (
7.1
) is taken as the form:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &X_{t}=X_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}V_{s}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}s,\\
&V_{t}=V_{0}+\int^{t}_{0}(K*f_{Z_{s}})(X_{s},V_{s}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}s+L^{(\alpha)}_{t},\end{aligned}\right.

(7.4)

where
f_{Z_{s}}
stands for the distribution density of
Z_{s}=(X_{s},V_{s})
.
By Theorem
1.3
we have

Theorem 7.2
.

Let
s\in(0,1]
,
\beta_{0}\in(-\alpha,0)
,
{\boldsymbol{p}}_{0}=(p_{0x},p_{0v})\in[1,\infty]^{2}

and
(X_{0},V_{0})\sim\mu_{0}\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d})
.
Suppose that

\displaystyle(1+\alpha)\tfrac{d}{p_{0x}}<\alpha+(2s-1-\tfrac{d}{p_{0v}})\wedge 0+\beta_{0}+\beta_{0}\wedge(-1).

(7.5)

If
\mu_{0}\in{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta_{0}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{0};{\boldsymbol{a}}}
satisfies the following smallness condition:
for some
C_{0}=C_{0}(s,\alpha,d,{\boldsymbol{p}}_{0},\beta_{0})>0
,

\displaystyle\|\mu_{0}\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta_{0}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{0};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\leqslant C_{0},

(7.6)

then there is a unique weak solution
Z_{t}=(X_{t},V_{t})
to SDE (
7.4
) in the class that
for each
t>0
,
Z_{t}
admits a smooth density
f(t)
so that for any
T>0
and
\beta\geqslant 0
,

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in(0,T]}\left(t^{(\beta-\beta_{0})/\alpha}\|f(t)\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta,1}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{0};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\right)<\infty.

(7.7)

Moreover, if
{\boldsymbol{p}}_{0}=(p_{0},p_{0})
, then we can drop (
7.6
).

Proof.

Under (
7.5
), one can choose
\beta_{b}<(2s-1-\frac{d}{p_{0v}})\wedge 0
and
\varrho_{0}=(1,\frac{p_{0v}}{p_{0v}-1})
so that

{\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot(\tfrac{d}{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{0}}+\tfrac{d}{\boldsymbol{\varrho}_{0}}-{\boldsymbol{d}})-\beta_{0}-\beta_{b}<\alpha+\beta_{0}\wedge(-1).

Thus by (
7.3
) and Theorem
1.3
, as in the proof of Theorem
5.2
, we conclude the proof.

Without (
7.6
), by Theorem
3.17
, there is a local solution to SDE (
7.4
) on some small time interval

[0,T_{0}]
, where
T_{0}
depends on the norm
\|\mu_{0}\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta_{0}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{0};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}
.
Next we want to extend the solution to arbitrary large time when
{\boldsymbol{p}}_{0}=(p_{0},p_{0})
.
Fix
t_{0}\in(0,T_{0})
. Let us start from time
t_{0}
to make some estimates.
Suppose that
\psi:[0,\infty]\to[0,\infty)
is a
C^{2}
-convex function.
Since
f\in C([t_{0},T_{0}];C^{2}_{b}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d}))
, by (
C.6
) we have

\int\psi(f(t))\leqslant\int\psi(f(t_{0}))-\int^{t}_{t_{0}}\int\nabla_{v}\Delta^{-s}_{v}f\cdot\nabla_{v}f\,\psi^{\prime\prime}(f)f,

where the integral
\int
is taken over
{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
.
Define

G(r):=\int^{r}_{0}s\psi^{\prime\prime}(s){\mathord{{\rm d}}}s.

It is easy to see that

\int\psi(f(t))\leqslant\int\psi(f(t_{0}))-\int^{t}_{t_{0}}\int\nabla_{v}\Delta^{-s}_{v}f\cdot\nabla_{v}G(f)=\int\psi(f(t_{0}))+\int^{t}_{t_{0}}\int\Delta^{1-s}_{v}f\,G(f).

By (
3.3
) and the change of variable, one sees that

\displaystyle\int\Delta^{1-s}_{v}f\,G(f)=\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\int_{|w|\geqslant\varepsilon}G(f(v))\frac{f(v+w)-f(v)}{|w|^{d+2(1-s)}}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}w{\mathord{{\rm d}}}v

\displaystyle\qquad=\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\frac{1}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\int_{|w|\geqslant\varepsilon}(G(f(v))-G(f(v+w)))\frac{f(v+w)-f(v)}{|w|^{d+2(1-s)}}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}w{\mathord{{\rm d}}}v

\displaystyle\qquad=-\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\frac{1}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\int_{|w|\geqslant\varepsilon}\int^{1}_{0}G^{\prime}(sf(v)+(1-s)f(v+w)){\mathord{{\rm d}}}s\frac{(f(v+w)-f(v))^{2}}{|w|^{d+2(1-s)}}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}w{\mathord{{\rm d}}}v.

Since
G^{\prime}(r)=r\psi^{\prime\prime}(r)\geqslant 0
for
r\geqslant 0
, we get

\int\Delta^{1-s}_{v}f\,G(f)\leqslant 0.

Therefore,

\int\psi(f(t))\leqslant\int\psi(f(t_{0})).

In particular,

\|f(t)\|_{p_{0}}\leqslant\|f(t_{0})\|_{p_{0}}.

As in the proof of Theorem
3.19
, we can extend the solution to arbitrary time.
∎

Appendix A
Several technical lemmas

The following two elementary lemmas are used to show the Schauder estimate in time-weighted spaces for kinetic semigroups.

Lemma A.1
.

For any
\gamma>\beta>0
, there is a constant
C=C(\gamma,\beta)\geqslant 1
such that for all
\lambda>0
,

\sum_{j\geqslant 1}2^{j\beta}((2^{-j\gamma}\lambda)\wedge 1)\asymp_{C}\lambda^{\frac{\beta}{\gamma}}\int^{\lambda}_{0}t^{-\frac{\beta}{\gamma}-1}(t\wedge 1){\mathord{{\rm d}}}t.

Proof.

Noting that for
j\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
s\in[j-1,j]
,

2^{s\beta}((2^{-(s+1)\gamma}\lambda)\wedge 1)\leqslant 2^{j\beta}((2^{-j\gamma}\lambda)\wedge 1)\leqslant 2^{(s+1)\beta}((2^{-s\gamma}\lambda)\wedge 1),

we have

\displaystyle{\mathscr{I}}_{0}:=\int^{\infty}_{0}2^{s\beta}((2^{-(s+1)\gamma}\lambda)\wedge 1){\mathord{{\rm d}}}s\leqslant\sum_{j\geqslant 1}2^{j\beta}((2^{-j\gamma}\lambda)\wedge 1)\leqslant\int^{\infty}_{0}2^{(s+1)\beta}((2^{-s\gamma}\lambda)\wedge 1){\mathord{{\rm d}}}s=:{\mathscr{I}}_{1}.

By the change of variables, we have

{\mathscr{I}}_{0}=\frac{\lambda^{\frac{\beta}{\gamma}}2^{-\beta}}{\gamma\ln 2}\int^{\lambda}_{0}t^{-\frac{\beta}{\gamma}-1}(t\wedge 1){\mathord{{\rm d}}}t,\ \ {\mathscr{I}}_{1}=\frac{\lambda^{\frac{\beta}{\gamma}}2^{\beta}}{\gamma\ln 2}\int^{\lambda}_{0}t^{-\frac{\beta}{\gamma}-1}(t\wedge 1){\mathord{{\rm d}}}t.

Thus we obtain the desired estimate.
∎

Lemma A.2
.

For any
\vartheta\in[0,\infty)
and
\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1}\in(-\infty,1)
,
there is a constant
C=C(\vartheta,\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1})>0
such that for all
\lambda,t>0
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\left[(\lambda(t-s))^{-\vartheta}\wedge 1\right](1\wedge s)^{-\gamma_{0}}(1\vee s)^{-\gamma_{1}}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}s\leqslant C\ell_{\vartheta}(\lambda t)(1\wedge t)^{1-\gamma_{0}}(1\vee t)^{1-\gamma_{1}},

where

\ell_{\vartheta}(\lambda):=(\lambda\vee 1)^{-\vartheta\wedge 1}(1+{\mathbbm{1}}_{\vartheta=1}\ln(1\vee\lambda)).

Proof.

We first show the following estimate: for any
\vartheta\in[0,\infty)
and
\gamma<1
,

\displaystyle{\mathscr{I}}(\lambda,t):=\int_{0}^{t}\left[(\lambda(t-s))^{-\vartheta}\wedge 1\right]s^{-\gamma}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}s\lesssim\ell_{\vartheta}(\lambda t)t^{1-\gamma},\ \ \forall\lambda,t>0.

(A.1)

By the change of variable, we have

{\mathscr{I}}(\lambda,t)=t^{1-\gamma}{\mathscr{I}}(t\lambda,1).

So, it suffices to prove (
A.1
) for
t=1
. Note that

\displaystyle{\mathscr{I}}(\lambda,1)

\displaystyle\leqslant 2^{\gamma\vee 0}\int_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}\left[(\lambda s)^{-\vartheta}\wedge 1\right]{\mathord{{\rm d}}}s+\left[(\tfrac{\lambda}{2})^{-\vartheta}\wedge 1\right]\int_{\frac{1}{2}}^{1}(1-s)^{-\gamma}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}s

\displaystyle=2^{\gamma\vee 0}\lambda^{-1}\int_{0}^{\frac{\lambda}{2}}\left[s^{-\vartheta}\wedge 1\right]{\mathord{{\rm d}}}s+\left[(\tfrac{\lambda}{2})^{-\vartheta}\wedge 1\right]\int_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}s^{-\gamma}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}s.

If
0\leqslant\vartheta\not=1
, then

\displaystyle{\mathscr{I}}(\lambda,1)\lesssim\lambda^{-1}\wedge 1+\lambda^{-\vartheta}\wedge 1\leqslant 2(\lambda^{-(\vartheta\wedge 1)}\wedge 1)=2(\lambda\vee 1)^{-\vartheta\wedge 1}.

If
\vartheta=1
, then

{\mathscr{I}}(\lambda,1)\lesssim(\lambda^{-1}\wedge 1)\ln(1\vee\lambda)+\lambda^{-1}\wedge 1\lesssim(\lambda\vee 1)^{-1}\ln(1\vee\lambda).

Hence,

{\mathscr{I}}(\lambda,1)\lesssim\ell_{\vartheta}(\lambda).

For
t\in(0,2]
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\left[(\lambda(t-s))^{-\vartheta}\wedge 1\right](1\wedge s)^{-\gamma_{0}}(1\vee s)^{-\gamma_{1}}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}s

\displaystyle\qquad\lesssim\int_{0}^{t}\left[(\lambda(t-s))^{-\vartheta}\wedge 1\right]s^{-\gamma_{0}}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}s\lesssim_{C}\ell_{\vartheta}(\lambda t)t^{1-\gamma_{0}}.

For
t>2
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\left[(\lambda(t-s))^{-\vartheta}\wedge 1\right](1\wedge s)^{-\gamma_{0}}(1\vee s)^{-\gamma_{1}}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}s

\displaystyle\qquad\leqslant\int_{0}^{1}\left[(\lambda(t-s))^{-\vartheta}\wedge 1\right]s^{-\gamma_{0}}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}s+\int_{1}^{t}\left[(\lambda(t-s))^{-\vartheta}\wedge 1\right]s^{-\gamma_{1}}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}s

\displaystyle\qquad\leqslant\left[(\lambda(t-1))^{-\vartheta}\wedge 1\right]\int_{0}^{1}s^{-\gamma_{0}}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}s+\int_{0}^{t}\left[(\lambda(t-s))^{-\vartheta}\wedge 1\right]s^{-\gamma_{1}}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}s

\displaystyle\qquad\lesssim\left[(\lambda t)^{-\vartheta}\wedge 1\right]+\ell_{\vartheta}(\lambda t)t^{1-\gamma_{1}}\lesssim\ell_{\vartheta}(\lambda t)(1\vee t)^{1-\gamma_{1}}.

The proof is complete.
∎

Below we recall some well-known results about singular kernels mentioned in the introduction.
For the readers’ convenience, we give full proofs.

Lemma A.3
.

Let
\gamma\in(0,d)
and
K:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
be a measurable function with that for some
C>0
,

\displaystyle|K(x)|\leqslant C|x|^{\gamma-d},\ \ x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}.

(i)

Let
1<q<p<\infty
satisfy

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{p}+\tfrac{\gamma}{d}=\tfrac{1}{q}.

Then there is a constant
C=C(p,q,d,\gamma)>0
such that for any
f\in L^{q}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
,

\displaystyle\|K*f\|_{p}\leqslant C\|f\|_{q}.

(A.2)

(ii)

Let
p\in[1,d/\gamma)
and
\theta:=p\gamma/d
. Then there is a constant
C=C(p,d,\gamma)>0

such that for any
f\in L^{p}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})\cap L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
,

\displaystyle\|K*f\|_{\infty}\leqslant C\|f\|_{p}^{\theta}\|f\|_{\infty}^{1-\theta}.

(A.3)

(iii)

For any
p\in(\frac{d}{d-\gamma},\infty]
, it holds that

\displaystyle K\in{\mathbf{B}}^{d/p-d+\gamma,\infty}_{p},\ \ \nabla K\in{\mathbf{B}}^{d/p-d+\gamma-1,\infty}_{p}.

(A.4)

(iv)

If
K
is a
(d-\gamma)
-order smooth homogenous function on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
, i.e.,

K(\lambda x)=\lambda^{\gamma-d}K(x)
,
\lambda>0
,
then for any
p_{0}\in[1,\frac{d}{d-\gamma})
and
p_{1}\in(\frac{d}{d-\gamma},\infty]
, it holds that

\displaystyle K\in{\mathbf{B}}^{d/p_{0}-d+\gamma,\infty}_{p_{0}}+{\mathbf{B}}^{d/p_{0}-d+\gamma,\infty}_{p_{1}}.

(A.5)

(v)

Let
K(x)=\nabla|x|^{\gamma-d}
and
K_{\varepsilon}(x)=\nabla(|x|\vee\varepsilon)^{\gamma-d}
for
\varepsilon>0
. Then
for any
p\in(\frac{d}{d-\gamma},\infty]
and
r\in[1,\frac{d}{d-\gamma})
, there is constant
C=C(r,d,p,\gamma)>0

such that for all
\varepsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\|K-K_{\varepsilon}\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta,\infty}_{p}}\lesssim\varepsilon^{\frac{d}{r}-d+\gamma},\ \ \beta:=\tfrac{d}{p}-\tfrac{d}{r}.

(A.6)

Proof.

(i) Let
r=d/(d-\gamma)
. By
[
1
, Theorem 1.5]
, we have

\displaystyle\|K*f\|_{p}\lesssim\|K\|_{r,\infty}\|f\|_{q},

where

\displaystyle\|K\|_{r,\infty}:=\sup_{\lambda>0}\lambda^{r}m\big\{x:|K(x)|>\lambda\big\}\lesssim\sup_{\lambda>0}\lambda^{r}m\big\{x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}:C|x|^{\gamma-d}>\lambda\big\}<\infty.

(ii) Let
p^{\prime}=p/(p-1)
. For any
\lambda>0
, by Hölder’s inequality we have

\displaystyle|K*f(x)|

\displaystyle\leqslant\int_{|x-y|\lesssim\lambda}\frac{|f(y)|}{|x-y|^{d-\gamma}}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}y+\int_{|x-y|>\lambda}\frac{|f(y)|}{|x-y|^{d-\gamma}}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}y

\displaystyle\lesssim\|f\|_{\infty}\lambda^{\gamma}+\|f\|_{p}\left(\int_{|x-y|>\lambda}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{(d-\gamma)p^{\prime}}}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}y\right)^{1/p^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|f\|_{\infty}\lambda^{\gamma}+\|f\|_{p}\lambda^{d/p^{\prime}-d+\gamma},

In particular, taking
\lambda=\|f\|_{p}^{p/d}\|f\|_{\infty}^{-p/d}
, we get

\displaystyle|K*f(x)|\lesssim\|f\|_{p}^{\theta}\|f\|_{\infty}^{1-\theta}.

(iii) For
p\in(\frac{d}{d-\gamma},\infty)
, letting
\tfrac{1}{p}+\tfrac{\gamma}{d}=\tfrac{1}{q}
,
by (
A.2
) we have for any
j\geqslant 1
,

\|{\mathcal{R}}_{j}K\|_{p}\lesssim\|\check{\phi}_{j}\|_{q}\lesssim 2^{j(d-\frac{d}{q})}\|\check{\phi}_{1}\|_{q}=2^{j(d-\frac{d}{p}-\gamma)}\|\check{\phi}_{1}\|_{q}.

For
p=\infty
, by (
A.3
) we have

\|{\mathcal{R}}_{j}K\|_{\infty}\leqslant\|\check{\phi}_{j}\|^{\frac{\gamma}{d}}_{1}\|\check{\phi}_{j}\|^{1-\frac{\gamma}{d}}_{\infty}=\|\check{\phi}_{1}\|^{\frac{\gamma}{d}}_{1}\|\check{\phi}_{1}\|^{1-\frac{\gamma}{d}}_{\infty}2^{j(d-\gamma)}.

Hence,
K\in{\mathbf{B}}^{d/p-d+\gamma,\infty}_{p}
. By Bernstein’s inequality, we have

\nabla K\in{\mathbf{B}}^{d/p-d+\gamma-1,\infty}_{p}
.

(iv) Now, let
\chi
be a smooth function with
\chi=1
on
B_{1}

and
\chi=0
on
B^{c}_{2}
. Define

K_{1}(x):=\chi(x)K(x),\ \ K_{2}(x):=(1-\chi(x))K(x).

Fix
p_{0}\in[1,\frac{d}{d-\gamma})
and
p_{1}\in(\frac{d}{d-\gamma},\infty]
.
It is easy to see that for any
m\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
,

\nabla^{m}K_{2}\in L^{p_{1}}\Rightarrow K_{2}\in{\mathbf{B}}^{d/p_{0}-d+\gamma,\infty}_{p_{1}}.

Next we show
K_{1}\in{\mathbf{B}}^{d/p_{0}-d+\gamma,\infty}_{p_{0}}
. Note that

\|{\mathcal{R}}_{0}K_{1}\|_{p_{0}}\leqslant C\|K_{1}\|_{p_{0}}<\infty.

For
j\geqslant 1
, since
K_{1}=K-K_{2}
, by
[
1
, Proposition 2.21]
we have

\|{\mathcal{R}}_{j}K_{1}\|_{p_{0}}\leqslant\|{\mathcal{R}}_{j}K\|_{p_{0}}+\|{\mathcal{R}}_{j}K_{2}\|_{p_{0}}\leqslant C2^{j(d-\gamma-\frac{d}{p_{0}})}+\|{\mathcal{R}}_{j}K_{2}\|_{p_{0}},

and

\displaystyle\|{\mathcal{R}}_{j}K_{2}\|_{p_{0}}

\displaystyle=\|\Delta^{-d}{\mathcal{R}}_{j}\Delta^{d}K_{2}\|_{p_{0}}\lesssim 2^{-2dj}\|\Delta^{d}K_{2}\|_{p_{0}}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{j(d-\gamma-\frac{d}{p_{0}})}\Big(\|1_{1\leqslant|x|\leqslant 2}G\|_{p_{0}}+\|1_{|x|\geqslant 1}\nabla^{2d}K\|_{p_{0}}\Big),

where
G
is a bounded function on
\{x:1\leqslant|x|\leqslant 2\}
. Hence,

\|K_{1}\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{d/p_{0}-d+\gamma,\infty}_{p_{0}}}=\sup_{j\geqslant 0}2^{j(\frac{d}{p_{0}}-d+\gamma)}\|{\mathcal{R}}_{j}K_{1}\|_{p_{0}}<\infty.

(v) For
p\in(\frac{d}{d-\gamma},\infty)
, letting
1+\tfrac{1}{p}=\tfrac{1}{r}+\tfrac{1}{q}
, by Young’s inequality we have

\|{\mathcal{R}}_{j}(K-K_{\varepsilon})\|_{p}\leqslant\|\nabla\check{\phi}_{j}\|_{q}\||\cdot|^{\gamma-d}-(|\cdot|\vee\varepsilon)^{\gamma-d}\|_{r}\lesssim 2^{j(d-\frac{d}{q})}\varepsilon^{\frac{d}{r}-d+\gamma}.

This completes the proof.
∎

We also need the following simple lifting proposition.

Proposition A.4
.

Suppose
K\in{\mathbf{B}}^{s,q}_{\varrho}
for some
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
q,\varrho\in[1,\infty]
. Let

K_{1}(x,v)=K(x),\ K_{2}(x,v)=K(v),\ \boldsymbol{\varrho}_{1}=(\varrho,\infty),\ \boldsymbol{\varrho}_{2}=(\infty,\varrho).

Then we have

\|K_{1}\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{(1+\alpha)s,q}_{\boldsymbol{\varrho}_{1};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\asymp\|K\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{s,q}_{\varrho}}\asymp\|K_{2}\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{s,q}_{\boldsymbol{\varrho}_{2};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}.

Proof.

We only prove
\|K_{1}\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{(1+\alpha)s,q}_{\boldsymbol{\varrho}_{1};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\asymp\|K\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{s,q}_{\varrho}}
. By definition we have for
j\geqslant 0
,

{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}K_{1}(x,v)=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}\check{\phi}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}(x-x^{\prime},v-v^{\prime})K(x^{\prime}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}x^{\prime}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}v^{\prime}=h_{j}*K(x),

where

h_{j}(x):=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\check{\phi}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}(x,v){\mathord{{\rm d}}}v.

Note that for
j\geqslant 1
,

\hat{h}_{j}(\xi)=2^{j(1+\alpha)d}(2\pi)^{-d}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}\mathrm{e}^{ix\cdot\xi}\check{\phi}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{1}(2^{j(1+\alpha)}x,v){\mathord{{\rm d}}}x{\mathord{{\rm d}}}v=\phi^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{1}(2^{-(1+\alpha)j}\xi,0),

where the support of
\phi^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{1}(2^{-(1+\alpha)j}\cdot,0)
is contained in

\displaystyle\Big\{2^{j-1}<|\xi|^{1/(1+\alpha)}<2^{j+1}\Big\}=\Big\{2^{(1+\alpha)(j-1)}<|\xi|<2^{(1+\alpha)(j+1)}\Big\}.

In particular, an equivalent norm of the Besov space
{\mathbf{B}}^{s,q}_{\varrho}
is then given by

\|f\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{s,q}_{\varrho}}\asymp\left(\sum_{j\geqslant 0}\big(2^{j(1+\alpha)s}\|h_{j}*f\|_{\varrho}\big)^{q}\right)^{1/q}.

Hence,

\|K_{1}\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{s,q}_{\boldsymbol{\varrho}_{1};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}^{q}=\sum_{j\geqslant 0}2^{j(1+\alpha)sq}\|{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}K_{1}\|_{\boldsymbol{\varrho}_{1}}^{q}=\sum_{j\geqslant 0}\big(2^{j(1+\alpha)s}\|h_{j}*K\|_{\varrho}\big)^{q}\asymp\|K\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{s,q}_{\varrho}}^{q}.

The proof is complete.
∎

Appendix B
Basic properties of Besov spaces

Proof of Lemma
2.6
.

(i) By definition we have

\|f\|_{\boldsymbol{p}}\leqslant\sum_{j\geqslant 0}\|{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}f\|_{\boldsymbol{p}}=\|f\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{0,1}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}

and

\|f\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{0,\infty}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}=\sup_{j\geqslant 0}\|{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}f\|_{\boldsymbol{p}}\lesssim\|f\|_{\boldsymbol{p}}.

(ii) For
j\geqslant 0
, by (
2.5
) and Young’s inequality, we have

\|{\mathcal{R}}^{{\boldsymbol{a}}}_{j}(f*g)\|_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}=\|({\mathcal{R}}^{{\boldsymbol{a}}}_{j}f)*(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{{\boldsymbol{a}}}_{j}g)\|_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\leqslant\|{\mathcal{R}}^{{\boldsymbol{a}}}_{j}f\|_{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{1}}\|\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{{\boldsymbol{a}}}_{j}g\|_{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{2}},

where
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{{\boldsymbol{a}}}_{j}g=\sum_{|i-j|\leqslant 2}{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{i}g
.
Hence,

\displaystyle\|f*g\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta,q}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{j}(2^{j\beta}\|{\mathcal{R}}^{{\boldsymbol{a}}}_{j}(f*g)\|_{{\boldsymbol{p}}})^{q}\right)^{1/q}

\displaystyle\leqslant\left(\sum_{j}(2^{j\beta_{1}}\|{\mathcal{R}}^{{\boldsymbol{a}}}_{j}f\|_{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{1}}2^{j\beta_{2}}\|\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{{\boldsymbol{a}}}_{j}g\|_{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{2}})^{q}\right)^{1/q}

\displaystyle\leqslant 5\|f\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta_{1},q_{1}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{1};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\|g\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta_{2},q_{2}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{2};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}.

(iii) For
j\geqslant 0
, we define
S_{j}=\sum_{k\leqslant j-1}{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}
with convention
{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{-1}=0
and
S_{-1}=0
. Recall the following Bony decomposition:

\displaystyle fg=f\prec g+f\circ g+g\prec f,

where

\displaystyle f\prec g:=\sum_{j\geqslant 0}S_{j-1}f{\mathcal{R}}^{{\boldsymbol{a}}}_{j}g\quad\text{and}\quad f\circ g:=\sum_{j\geqslant 0}{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}f\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{{\boldsymbol{a}}}_{j}g.

Here
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}
is defined in (
2.5
).
Noting that

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}}_{k}{\big(}S_{j-1}f{\mathcal{R}}^{{\boldsymbol{a}}}_{j}g{\big)}=0,\quad\forall|k-j|>3,

(B.1)

we have for
k\geqslant 0
,

\displaystyle\|{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{k}(f\prec g)\|_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\leqslant\sum_{j\geqslant 0}\|{\mathcal{R}}_{k}{\big(}S_{j-1}f{\mathcal{R}}^{{\boldsymbol{a}}}_{j}g{\big)}\|_{\boldsymbol{p}}=\sum_{|j-k|\leqslant 3}\|{\mathcal{R}}_{k}^{\boldsymbol{a}}{\big(}S_{j-1}f{\mathcal{R}}^{{\boldsymbol{a}}}_{j}g{\big)}\|_{\boldsymbol{p}}

\displaystyle\quad\lesssim\sum_{|j-k|\leqslant 3}\|S_{j-1}f{\mathcal{R}}^{{\boldsymbol{a}}}_{j}g\|_{\boldsymbol{p}}\leqslant\sum_{|j-k|\leqslant 3}\|S_{j-1}f\|_{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{1}}\|{\mathcal{R}}^{{\boldsymbol{a}}}_{j}g\|_{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{2}}\lesssim\|f\|_{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{1}}\sum_{|j-k|\leqslant 3}\|{\mathcal{R}}^{{\boldsymbol{a}}}_{j}g\|_{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{2}}.

Hence,

\displaystyle\|f\prec g\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{s,q}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|f\|_{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{1}}\left(\sum_{k\geqslant-1}2^{skq}\sum_{|j-k|\leqslant 3}\|{\mathcal{R}}^{{\boldsymbol{a}}}_{j}g\|^{q}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{2}}\right)^{1/q}\lesssim\|f\|_{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{1}}\|g\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{s,q}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{2};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}.

By symmetry, we also have

\|f\prec g\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{s}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\lesssim\|f\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{s,q}_{p_{1};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\|g\|_{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{2}}.

Moreover, noting that

{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{k}({\mathcal{R}}^{{\boldsymbol{a}}}_{j}f\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{{\boldsymbol{a}}}_{j}g)=0,\ \ k>j+5,

for
k>5
we have

\displaystyle\|{\mathcal{R}}^{{\boldsymbol{a}}}_{k}(f\circ g)\|_{\boldsymbol{p}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{j\geqslant k-5}\|{\mathcal{R}}^{{\boldsymbol{a}}}_{k}({\mathcal{R}}^{{\boldsymbol{a}}}_{j}f\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{{\boldsymbol{a}}}_{j}g)\|_{\boldsymbol{p}}\lesssim\sum_{j\geqslant k-5}\|{\mathcal{R}}^{{\boldsymbol{a}}}_{j}f\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{{\boldsymbol{a}}}_{j}g\|_{\boldsymbol{p}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{j\geqslant k-5}\|{\mathcal{R}}^{{\boldsymbol{a}}}_{j}f\|_{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{1}}\|\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{{\boldsymbol{a}}}_{j}g\|_{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{2}}\lesssim\sum_{j\geqslant k-5}\|{\mathcal{R}}^{{\boldsymbol{a}}}_{j}f\|_{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{1}}\|g\|_{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{2}}.

Since
s>0
, by Young’s inequality for discrete summation, we get

\|f\circ g\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{s,q}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\lesssim\left(\sum_{k\geqslant 0}2^{skq}\left(\sum_{j\geqslant k-5}\|{\mathcal{R}}^{{\boldsymbol{a}}}_{j}f\|_{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{1}}\right)^{q}\right)^{1/q}\|g\|_{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{2}}\lesssim\|f\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{s,q}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{1};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\|g\|_{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{2}}.

Combining the above estimates, we complete the proof of (iii).

(iv)
For
\theta=0,1
, (
2.16
) is direct by (
2.7
). Below we assume
\theta\in(0,1)
.
Let
{\boldsymbol{p}}_{2}\leqslant{\boldsymbol{w}}\in[1,\infty]^{N}
and
s_{2}\in{\mathbb{R}}
be defined by

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}=\tfrac{1-\theta}{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{1}}+\tfrac{\theta}{{\boldsymbol{w}}},\ \ s_{1}(1-\theta)+s_{3}\theta=s.

(B.2)

By Hölder’s inequality, we have

\displaystyle\|{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}f\|_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}

\displaystyle=\|({\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}f)^{1-\theta}({\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}f)^{\theta}\|_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\leqslant\|{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}f\|^{1-\theta}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{1}}\|{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}f\|^{\theta}_{{\boldsymbol{w}}}.

Hence,

\displaystyle 2^{js}\|{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}f\|_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\Big(2^{js_{1}}\|{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}f\|_{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{1}}\Big)^{1-\theta}\Big(2^{js_{3}}\|{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}f\|_{{\boldsymbol{w}}}\Big)^{\theta}.

(B.3)

Note that by (
2.15
) and (
B.2
),

s_{2}=s_{3}+{\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot(\tfrac{d}{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{2}}-\tfrac{d}{{\boldsymbol{w}}}),

and by (
2.7
),

\|{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}f\|_{{\boldsymbol{w}}}\lesssim 2^{j{\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot(\frac{d}{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{2}}-\frac{d}{{\boldsymbol{w}}})}\|{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}f\|_{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{2}},\ j\geqslant 0.

Substituting it into (
B.3
), we obtain

2^{js}\|{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}f\|_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\lesssim\Big(2^{js_{1}}\|{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}f\|_{{\boldsymbol{q}}}\Big)^{1-\theta}\Big(2^{js_{2}}\|{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}f\|_{{\boldsymbol{q}}}\Big)^{\theta}.

Thus we obtain (iv) by Hölder’s inequality with respect to
j
.

(v) Note that for
\xi=(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})
,

\widehat{{\mathcal{R}}^{x}_{k}{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}f}(\xi)=\phi_{k}(\xi_{1})\phi^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}(\xi)\hat{f}(\xi).

It is easy to see that

\phi_{k}(\xi_{1})\phi^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}(\xi)=0,\ \ k>(1+\alpha)(j+2).

Hence,

\displaystyle\sup_{k,j\geqslant 1}2^{\frac{ks_{0}}{1+\alpha}}2^{js_{1}}\|{\mathcal{R}}^{x}_{k}{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}f\|_{\boldsymbol{p}}

\displaystyle=\sup_{k\leqslant(1+\alpha)(j+2)}2^{\frac{ks_{0}}{1+\alpha}}2^{js_{1}}\|{\mathcal{R}}^{x}_{k}{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}f\|_{\boldsymbol{p}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sup_{k\leqslant(1+\alpha)(j+2)}2^{\frac{k(s_{0}-\varepsilon)}{1+\alpha}}2^{j(s_{1}+\varepsilon)}\|{\mathcal{R}}^{x}_{k}{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}f\|_{\boldsymbol{p}}.

(vi) By definition and Bernstein’s inequality we have

\displaystyle\|f\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\theta\gamma,(1-\theta)\beta}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};x,{\boldsymbol{a}}}}

\displaystyle=\sup_{k,j\geqslant 0}\left(2^{\frac{k\gamma}{1+\alpha}}\|{\mathcal{R}}^{x}_{k}{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}f\|_{\boldsymbol{p}}\right)^{\theta}\left(2^{j\beta}\|{\mathcal{R}}^{x}_{k}{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}f\|_{\boldsymbol{p}}\right)^{1-\theta}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sup_{k,j\geqslant 0}\left(2^{\frac{k\gamma}{1+\alpha}}\|{\mathcal{R}}^{x}_{k}f\|_{\boldsymbol{p}}\right)^{\theta}\left(2^{j\beta}\|{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}f\|_{\boldsymbol{p}}\right)^{1-\theta}=\|f\|^{\theta}_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\gamma/(1+\alpha)}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};x}}\|f\|^{1-\theta}_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}.

The proof is complete.
∎

Appendix C
A priori
L^{1}
-estimates for kinetic equation

In this appendix we use the probabilistic representation to show some a priori estimates for linear kinetic Fokker-Planck equation
with smooth coefficients, which has independent interest.

Lemma C.1
.

Let
T>0
and
\alpha\in(0,2]
. Let
{H}\in L^{1}([0,T];C^{1}_{b}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d}))
and

u\in C([0,T];C^{2}_{b}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d}))
be a smooth solution of the linear Fokker-Planck equation

\partial_{t}u=\Delta^{\alpha/2}_{v}u-v\cdot\nabla_{x}u-\mathord{{\rm div}}_{v}({H}u).

For any
t\in[0,T]
, it holds that

\displaystyle\|u(t)\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\infty}}\leqslant\|u(0)\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\infty}}\mathrm{e}^{\int^{t}_{0}\|(\mathord{{\rm div}}_{v}{H}_{s})^{-}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\infty}}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}s}

(C.1)

and

\displaystyle\|u(t)\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{1}}\leqslant\|u(0)\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{1}}.

(C.2)

Proof.

(i) Fix
t_{0}\in(0,T]
. Let
{\mathbf{H}}_{t}(z):={\mathbf{H}}_{t}(x,v):=(-v,-{H}_{t}(x,v))
and
\sigma:=(0,{\mathbb{I}})^{*}
.
Let
Z_{t}(z)=Z_{t}
solve the following SDE

{\mathord{{\rm d}}}Z_{t}={\mathbf{H}}_{t_{0}-t}(Z_{t}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}t+\sigma{\mathord{{\rm d}}}L^{(\alpha)}_{t},\ \ Z_{0}=z,\ \ t\in[0,t_{0}].

Since we are considering the additive noise and
{H}\in L^{1}([0,T];C^{1}_{b}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d}))
, it is easy to see that

z\mapsto Z_{t}(z)\mbox{ is a $C^{1}$-diffeomorphism,}

and

\nabla Z_{t}(z)={\mathbb{I}}_{2d\times 2d}+\int^{t}_{0}\nabla{\mathbf{H}}_{t_{0}-s}(Z_{s}(z))\nabla Z_{s}(z){\mathord{{\rm d}}}s.

Let
G_{t}
be the inverse of matrix
\nabla Z_{t}(z)
, which solves the following ODE

\displaystyle G_{t}(z)={\mathbb{I}}_{2d\times 2d}-\int^{t}_{0}G_{s}(z)\cdot\nabla{\mathbf{H}}_{t_{0}-s}(Z_{s}(z)){\mathord{{\rm d}}}s.

(C.3)

Note that

\partial_{t}u=\Delta^{\alpha/2}_{v}u-v\cdot\nabla_{x}u-{H}\cdot\nabla_{v}u-\mathord{{\rm div}}_{v}{H}u.

By applying Itô’s formula to the function
(s,z)\mapsto u(t_{0}-s,z)
, we have

u(t_{0}-t,Z_{t}(z))=u(t_{0},z)-\int^{t}_{0}(\mathord{{\rm div}}_{v}{H}u)(t_{0}-s,Z_{s}(z)){\mathord{{\rm d}}}s+\mbox{a martingale}.

Using product Itô’s formula we get

{\mathbb{E}}\left(u(t_{0}-t,Z_{t}(z))\mathrm{e}^{-\int^{t}_{0}\mathord{{\rm div}}_{v}{H}_{t_{0}-s}(Z_{s}(z)){\mathord{{\rm d}}}s}\right)=u(t_{0},z).

Hence,

|u(t_{0},z)|\leqslant\|u(0)\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\infty}}\mathrm{e}^{\int^{t_{0}}_{0}\|(\mathord{{\rm div}}_{v}{H}_{t_{0}-s})^{-}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\infty}}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}s}.

Replacing
t_{0}
by
t
, we obtain (
C.1
). Moreover, by the change of variables, we also have

\displaystyle\|u(t_{0})\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{1}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathrm{e}^{\int^{t_{0}}_{0}\|(\mathord{{\rm div}}_{v}{H}_{t_{0}-s})^{-}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\infty}}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}s}{\mathbb{E}}\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}|u(0,Z_{t_{0}}(z))|{\mathord{{\rm d}}}z\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathrm{e}^{\int^{t_{0}}_{0}\|(\mathord{{\rm div}}_{v}{H}_{t_{0}-s})^{-}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\infty}}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}s}{\mathbb{E}}\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}|u(0,z)|\det(\nabla Z^{-1}_{t_{0}}(z)){\mathord{{\rm d}}}z\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathrm{e}^{\int^{t_{0}}_{0}\|(\mathord{{\rm div}}_{v}{H}_{t_{0}-s})^{-}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\infty}}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}s}\sup_{z}{\mathbb{E}}(\det G_{t_{0}}(z))\|u(0)\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{1}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathrm{e}^{\int^{t_{0}}_{0}(\|(\mathord{{\rm div}}_{v}{H}_{t_{0}-s})^{-}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\infty}}+\|(\mathord{{\rm div}}_{v}{H}_{t_{0}-s})^{+}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\infty}}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}s}\|u(0)\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{1}}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{\int^{t_{0}}_{0}\|\mathord{{\rm div}}_{v}{H}_{s}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\infty}}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}s}\|u(0)\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{1}},

(C.4)

where we have used that
\nabla Z^{-1}_{t_{0}}(z)=G_{t_{0}}\circ Z^{-1}_{t_{0}}(z)
in the third inequality, and the last inequality is due to
(cf.
[
60
]
)

\det G_{t}(z)=1-\int^{t}_{0}\det G_{s}(z)\cdot\mathord{{\rm div}}_{v}H_{t_{0}-s}(Z_{s}(z)){\mathord{{\rm d}}}s.

(ii) Let
\psi:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be a smooth convex function with

\displaystyle|\psi(r)|+|\psi^{\prime}(r)|\leqslant Cr,\ \ |\psi^{\prime\prime}(r)|\leqslant C.

(C.5)

Let
\chi\in C^{\infty}_{c}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
be a cutoff function in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
with
\chi(v)=1
for
|v|\leqslant 1
and

\chi(v)=0
for
|v|>2
. For
R>0
, define

\chi_{R}(v):=\chi(v/R).

By the chain rule we have

\partial_{t}\psi(u)\chi_{R}=\psi^{\prime}(u)\Delta^{\alpha/2}_{v}u\chi_{R}-v\cdot\nabla_{x}\psi(u)\chi_{R}-\mathord{{\rm div}}_{v}({H}u)\psi^{\prime}(u)\chi_{R}.

Integrating both sides over
[0,t]\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
, we get

\int\psi(u(t))\chi_{R}=\int\psi(u(0))\chi_{R}+\int^{t}_{0}\int\Delta^{\alpha/2}_{v}u\psi^{\prime}(u)\chi_{R}-\int^{t}_{0}\int\mathord{{\rm div}}_{v}({H}u)\psi^{\prime}(u)\chi_{R},

where we have used that

\int v\cdot\nabla_{x}\psi(u)\chi_{R}=\int\mathord{{\rm div}}_{x}((v\chi_{R})\psi(u))=0.

By (
C.4
) and (
C.5
), letting
R\to\infty
and by the dominated convergence theorem, we get

\int\psi(u(t))=\int\psi(u(0))+\int^{t}_{0}\int\Delta^{\alpha/2}_{v}u\psi^{\prime}(u)-\int^{t}_{0}\int\mathord{{\rm div}}_{v}({H}u)\psi^{\prime}(u).

We consider the second term in the right hand side. For
\alpha=2
, we directly have

\int\Delta_{v}u\psi^{\prime}(u)=-\int|\nabla_{v}u|^{2}\psi^{\prime\prime}(u)\leqslant 0.

For
\alpha\in(0,2)
, by (
3.3
) and symmetry we have

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\Delta^{\alpha/2}_{v}u(v)\psi^{\prime}(u(v)){\mathord{{\rm d}}}v=\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\int_{|w|\geqslant\varepsilon}\psi^{\prime}(u(v))\frac{u(v+w)-u(v)}{|w|^{d+\alpha}}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}w{\mathord{{\rm d}}}v

\displaystyle\qquad=\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\frac{1}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\int_{|w|\geqslant\varepsilon}(\psi^{\prime}(u(v))-\psi^{\prime}(u(v+w)))\frac{u(v+w)-u(v)}{|w|^{d+\alpha}}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}w{\mathord{{\rm d}}}v

\displaystyle\qquad=-\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\frac{1}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\int_{|w|\geqslant\varepsilon}\int^{1}_{0}\psi^{\prime\prime}(su(v)+(1-s)u(v+w)){\mathord{{\rm d}}}s\frac{(u(v+w)-u(v))^{2}}{|w|^{d+\alpha}}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}w{\mathord{{\rm d}}}v\leqslant 0.

Hence,

\displaystyle\int\psi(u(t))\leqslant\int\psi(u(0))+\int^{t}_{0}\int({H}u\cdot\nabla_{v}u)\psi^{\prime\prime}(u).

(C.6)

Now we take

\psi(r)=\psi_{\delta}(r):=\sqrt{r^{2}+\delta}-\sqrt{\delta},\ \ \delta>0.

Clearly,

\lim_{\delta\downarrow 0}\psi_{\delta}(r)=|r|,\ |\psi_{\delta}(r)|\leqslant|r|,\ \psi^{\prime}_{\delta}(r)=\frac{r}{\sqrt{r^{2}+\delta}},\ \ \psi^{\prime\prime}_{\delta}(r)=\frac{\delta}{(r^{2}+\delta)^{3/2}}.

By (
C.6
), we have

\int\psi_{\delta}(u(t))\leqslant\int\psi_{\delta}(u(0))+\int^{t}_{0}\int({H}u\cdot\nabla_{v}u)\psi^{\prime\prime}_{\delta}(u).

Noting that

|\psi^{\prime\prime}_{\delta}(u)|\cdot|{H}|\cdot|u|\cdot|\nabla_{v}u|\leqslant|{H}|\cdot|\nabla_{v}u|,

by taking limits
\delta\to 0
and the dominated convergence theorem, we get

\displaystyle\int|u(t)|\leqslant\int|u(0)|+\lim_{\delta\to 0}\int^{t}_{0}\int|\psi^{\prime\prime}_{\delta}(u)|\cdot|{H}|\cdot|u|\cdot|\nabla_{v}u|=\int|u(0)|.

The proof is complete.
∎
